\mathbf{A}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
.

2.

Generate
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)
.

3.

Sample
\mathbf{u}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
and
r\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}
.

4.

Generate
\rho_{R,\mathbf{\textsc{Aux}}}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}|\otimes\nu_{\lambda})
.

5.

Run
{\sf IneffRevoke}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\sigma,\cdot)
on system
R
. If it outputs
\top
, continue. Otherwise, output
\mathsf{Invalid}
.

6.

Run
b^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{u},r,\cdot)
on system
Aux
. Output
b^{\prime}
.

Figure 11:
The distribution
{\sf simult}.{\sf unif}.{\sf Dist}^{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D}}\left(1^{\lambda}\right)
.

Proof.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
be the security parameter and let
\mathcal{A}=\{(\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}},\nu_{\lambda})\}_{\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}}

be a non-uniform quantum algorithm. Suppose that
\mathcal{D}
is a
\mathsf{QPT}
distinguisher with advantage
\varepsilon=1/\mathrm{poly}(\lambda)
.

To prove the claim, we consider the following sequence of hybrid distributions.

\mathsf{H}_{0}
: This is the distribution
{\sf simult}.{\sf lwe}.{\sf Dist}^{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D}}\left(1^{\lambda}\right)
in
Figure

10
.

\mathsf{H}_{1}
: This is the following distribution:

1.

Sample a random matrix
\mathbf{A}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
.

2.

Sample a Gaussian vector
\mathbf{x}_{0}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m},\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{2}}}
and let
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{A}\cdot\mathbf{x}_{0}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q)
.

3.

Run
\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}\leftarrow\mathsf{IneffQSampGauss}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\sigma)
.

4.

Sample
\mathbf{s}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
,
\mathbf{e}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z}^{m},\alpha q}
and
e^{\prime}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z},\beta q}
.
Let
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{s}+\mathbf{e}
.

5.

Run
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}|\otimes\nu_{\lambda})
to generate a state
\rho_{\textsc{R},\mathbf{\textsc{aux}}}
in systems
R
and
aux
.

6.

Run
{\sf IneffRevoke}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\sigma,\cdot)
on
R
. If it outputs
\top
, continue. Otherwise, output
\mathsf{Invalid}
.

7.

Run the distinguisher

\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}^{\intercal}\mathbf{x}_{0}+e^{\prime},\cdot)
on system
Aux
. Output
b^{\prime}
.

\mathsf{H}_{2}:
This is the following distribution:

1.

Sample a random matrix
\mathbf{A}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
.

2.

Sample a Gaussian vector
\mathbf{x}_{0}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m},\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{2}}}
and let
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{A}\cdot\mathbf{x}_{0}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q)
.

3.

Run
\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}\leftarrow\mathsf{IneffQSampGauss}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\sigma)
.

4.

Sample

\mathbf{u}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
and
e^{\prime}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z},\beta q}
.

5.

Run
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}|\otimes\nu_{\lambda})
to generate a state
\rho_{\textsc{R},\mathbf{\textsc{aux}}}
in systems
R
and
aux
.

6.

Run
{\sf IneffRevoke}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\sigma,\cdot)
on
R
. If it outputs
\top
, continue. Otherwise, output
\mathsf{Invalid}
.

7.

Run the distinguisher

\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}^{\intercal}\mathbf{x}_{0}+e^{\prime},\cdot)
on system
Aux
. Output
b^{\prime}
.

\mathsf{H}_{3}
: This is the distribution
{\sf simult}.{\sf unif}.{\sf Dist}^{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D}}\left(1^{\lambda}\right)
defined in
Figure

11
.

We now show the following:

Claim 6.17
.

Hybrids
\mathsf{H}_{0}
and
\mathsf{H}_{1}
are statistically indistinguishable. In other words,

\mathsf{H}_{0}\,\approx_{s}\,\mathsf{H}_{1}.

Proof.

Here, we invoke the
noise flooding
property in
Lemma

2.9
to argue that
\mathbf{e}^{\intercal}\mathbf{x}_{0}\ll e^{\prime}
holds with overwhelming probability for our choice of parameters.
Therefore, the distributions in
\mathsf{H}_{0}
and
\mathsf{H}_{1}
are computationally indistinguishable.
∎

Claim 6.18
.

Assuming
1
holds for our choice of parameters, the hybrids
\mathsf{H}_{1}
and
\mathsf{H}_{2}
are computationally indistinguishable,

\mathsf{H}_{1}\,\approx_{c}\,\mathsf{H}_{2}.

Proof.

This follows directly from
1
which allows us to invoke a variant of
\mathsf{LWE}
assumption, even if the procedure
{\sf IneffRevoke}
is applied on a separate register. Here, we rely on the fact that during the reduction, we can simply sample
e^{\prime}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z},\beta q}
to produce an identically distributed challenge distribution.
∎

Recall that
\mathsf{H}_{0}
and
\mathsf{H}_{3}
can be distinguished with probability
\varepsilon=1/\mathrm{poly}(\lambda)
. We proved that the hybrids
\mathsf{H}_{0}
and
\mathsf{H}_{2}
are computationally indistinguishable. As a consequence, it holds that hybrids
\mathsf{H}_{2}
and
\mathsf{H}_{3}
can be distinguished with probability at least
\varepsilon-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
.

We leverage this to obtain a Goldreich-Levin reduction. Consider the following distinguisher.

\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\big(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{u},v,\rho\big)
:

Input:
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
,
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
,
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
,
v\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}
and
\rho\in L(\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{\textsc{Aux}}})
.

Output: A bit
b^{\prime}\in{\{0,1\}}
.

Procedure:

1.

Sample
e^{\prime}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z},\beta q}
.

2.

Output
b^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathcal{D}\big(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{u},v+e^{\prime},\rho\big)
.

Figure 12:
The distinguisher
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\big(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{u},v,\rho\big)
.

Note that
r+e^{\prime}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q)
is uniform whenever
r\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}
and
e^{\prime}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z},\beta q}
. Therefore, our previous argument shows that there exists a negligible function
\eta
such that:

\displaystyle\vline\Pr\left[\begin{subarray}{c}{\sf IneffRevoke}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\sigma,\textsc{R})=\top\\
\ \\
\bigwedge\\
\ \\
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}^{\intercal}\mathbf{x}_{0},\textsc{Aux})=1\end{subarray}\,:\,\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{A}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m},\mathbf{u}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}\\
\mathbf{x}_{0}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m},\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{2}}},\,\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{0}\text{ (mod $q$)}\\
\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}\leftarrow\mathsf{IneffQSampGauss}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\sigma)\\
\rho_{\textsc{R},\mathbf{\textsc{Aux}}}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}|\otimes\nu_{\lambda})\end{subarray}\right]-

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\begin{subarray}{c}{\sf IneffRevoke}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\sigma,\textsc{R})=\top\\
\ \\
\bigwedge\\
\ \\
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{u},r,\textsc{Aux})=1\end{subarray}\,:\,\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{A}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}\\
\mathbf{u}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m},r\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}\\
\mathbf{x}_{0}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m},\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{2}}},\,\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{0}\text{ (mod $q$)}\\
\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}\leftarrow\mathsf{IneffQSampGauss}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\sigma)\\
\rho_{\textsc{R},\mathbf{\textsc{Aux}}}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}|\otimes\nu_{\lambda})\end{subarray}\right]\vline\geq\varepsilon-\eta(\lambda).

From
Theorem

4.3
, it follows that there exists a Goldreich-Levin extractor
\mathcal{E}
running in time
T(\mathcal{E})=\mathrm{poly}(\lambda,n,m,\sigma,q,1/\varepsilon)
that outputs a short vector in
\Lambda_{q}^{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{A})
with probability at least

\Pr\left[\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\rho_{\mathbf{\textsc{Aux}}})=\mathbf{x}\vskip 2.84526pt\\
\bigwedge\vskip 0.28453pt\\
{\mathbf{x}}\,\,\in\,\,\Lambda_{q}^{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{A})\,\cap\,\mathcal{B}^{m}(\mathbf{0},\sigma\sqrt{\frac{m}{2}})\end{subarray}\,:\,\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{A}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}\\
\mathbf{x}_{0}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m},\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{2}}},\,\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{0}\text{ (mod $q$)}\\
\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}\leftarrow\mathsf{IneffQSampGauss}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\sigma)\\
\rho_{\textsc{R},\mathbf{\textsc{Aux}}}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}|\otimes\nu_{\lambda})\end{subarray}\right]\geq\mathrm{poly}(\varepsilon,1/q).

This proves the claim.
∎

Finally, we give a proof of the
simultaneous
search-to-decision reduction (
Theorem

6.16
) from standard assumptions without
1
in the special case when revocation succeeds with overwhelming probability.

Theorem 6.19
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
q
be a prime with
q=2^{o(n)}
,
m\geq 2n\log q
,
\sqrt{8m}<\sigma<q/\sqrt{8m}
, and let
\alpha,\beta\in(0,1)
with
\beta/\alpha=2^{o(n)}
with
1/\alpha=2^{o(n)}\cdot\sigma
. Let

\mathcal{A}=\{(\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}},\nu_{\lambda})\}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{N}}

be any non-uniform quantum algorithm consisting of a family of polynomial-sized quantum circuits

\Bigg\{\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{q}^{m}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B_{\lambda}})\allowbreak\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{R_{\lambda}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\textsc{aux}_{\lambda}})\Bigg\}_{\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m},\,\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}}

and polynomial-sized advice states
\nu_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{B_{\lambda}})
which are independent of
\mathbf{A}
such that

\Pr\left[{\sf IneffRevoke}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\sigma,\rho_{\textsc{R}})=\top\,:\,\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{A}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}\\
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)\\
\rho_{\textsc{R},\mathbf{\textsc{Aux}}}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}|\otimes\nu_{\lambda})\end{subarray}\right]=1-\nu(\lambda),

for some negligle function
\nu(\lambda)
.
Then,
assuming the quantum hardness of the
\mathsf{LWE}_{n,q,\alpha q}^{m}
assumption, the following holds for every
\mathsf{QPT}
distinguisher
\mathcal{D}
. Suppose that there exists a function
\varepsilon(\lambda)=1/\mathrm{poly}(\lambda)
such that

\displaystyle\vline\,{\sf Pr}\left[1\leftarrow\mathsf{SimultSearchToDecisionExpt}^{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D}}(1^{\lambda},0)\right]-

\displaystyle\,{\sf Pr}\left[1\leftarrow\mathsf{SimultSearchToDecisionExpt}^{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D}}(1^{\lambda},1)\right]\,\vline=\varepsilon(\lambda).

Then, there exists a quantum extractor
\mathcal{E}
that takes as input
\mathbf{A}
,
\mathbf{y}
and system
Aux
of the state
\rho_{\textsc{R},\mathbf{\textsc{Aux}}}
and outputs a short vector in the coset
\Lambda_{q}^{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{A})
in time
\mathrm{poly}(\lambda,m,\sigma,q,1/\varepsilon)
such that

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\begin{subarray}{c}{\sf IneffRevoke}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\sigma,\textsc{R})=\top\vskip 0.56905pt\\
\bigwedge\vskip 0.56905pt\\
\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\textsc{Aux})\,\,\in\,\,\Lambda_{q}^{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{A})\,\cap\,\mathcal{B}^{m}(\mathbf{0},\sigma\sqrt{\frac{m}{2}})\end{subarray}\,:\,\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{A}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}\\
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)\\
\rho_{\textsc{R},\mathbf{\textsc{Aux}}}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}|\otimes\nu_{\lambda})\end{subarray}\right]\,\geq\,\mathrm{poly}(\varepsilon,1/q).

Proof.

By assumption, there exists an adversary
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D})
such that
\mathsf{Adv}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D})=\varepsilon(\lambda)
, where

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D})=\,

\displaystyle\vline\Pr\left[\begin{subarray}{c}{\sf IneffRevoke}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\sigma,\textsc{R})=\top\\
\ \\
\bigwedge\\
\ \\
\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{s}^{\intercal}\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{e}^{\intercal},\mathbf{s}^{\intercal}\mathbf{y}+e^{\prime},\textsc{Aux})=1\end{subarray}\,:\,\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{A}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m},\,\mathbf{s}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}\\
\mathbf{e}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z}^{m},\,\alpha q},\,e^{\prime}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z},\,\beta q}\\
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)\\
\rho_{\textsc{R},\mathbf{\textsc{Aux}}}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}|\otimes\nu_{\lambda})\end{subarray}\right]-

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\begin{subarray}{c}{\sf IneffRevoke}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\sigma,\textsc{R})=\top\\
\ \\
\bigwedge\\
\ \\
\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{u},r,\textsc{Aux})=1\end{subarray}\,:\,\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{A}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}\\
\mathbf{u}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m},r\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}\\
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)\\
\rho_{\textsc{R},\mathbf{\textsc{Aux}}}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}|\otimes\nu_{\lambda})\end{subarray}\right]\vline.

We can now invoke
Lemma

6.10
to argue that there exists a
\mathsf{QPT}
distinguisher
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}
(that internally runs
\mathcal{D}
) and succeeds on the reduced system
Aux
alone, i.e.

\displaystyle\vline\Pr\left[\tilde{\mathcal{D}}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{s}^{\intercal}\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{e}^{\intercal},\mathbf{s}^{\intercal}\mathbf{y}+e^{\prime},\rho_{\textsc{Aux}})=1\,:\,\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{A}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m},\,\mathbf{s}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}\\
\mathbf{e}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z}^{m},\,\alpha q},\,e^{\prime}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z},\,\beta q}\\
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)\\
\rho_{\textsc{R},\mathbf{\textsc{Aux}}}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}|\otimes\nu_{\lambda})\end{subarray}\right]-

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\tilde{\mathcal{D}}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{u},r,\rho_{\textsc{Aux}})=1\,:\,\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{A}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}\\
\mathbf{u}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m},r\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}\\
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)\\
\rho_{\textsc{R},\mathbf{\textsc{Aux}}}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}|\otimes\nu_{\lambda})\end{subarray}\right]\vline=\bar{\varepsilon}(\lambda),

for some
\bar{\varepsilon}=1/\mathrm{poly}(\lambda)
.
In other words, the
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithm
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}
can successfully predict whether it has received a Dual-Regev sample or a uniformly random sample.
Therefore, we can now invoke
Theorem

6.11
to argue there exists an extractor
\mathcal{E}
that takes as input
\mathbf{A}
,
\mathbf{y}
and system
Aux
of the state
\rho_{\textsc{R},\mathbf{\textsc{Aux}}}
and outputs a short vector in the coset
\Lambda_{q}^{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{A})
in time
\mathrm{poly}(\lambda,m,\sigma,q,1/\bar{\varepsilon})
such that

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\rho_{\mathbf{\textsc{Aux}}})=\mathbf{x}\vskip 2.84526pt\\
\bigwedge\vskip 0.28453pt\\
{\mathbf{x}}\,\,\in\,\,\Lambda_{q}^{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{A})\,\cap\,\mathcal{B}^{m}(\mathbf{0},\sigma\sqrt{\frac{m}{2}})\end{subarray}\,:\,\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{A}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}\\
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)\\
\rho_{\textsc{R},\mathbf{\textsc{Aux}}}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}|\otimes\nu_{\lambda})\end{subarray}\right]\geq\mathrm{poly}(\bar{\varepsilon},1/q).

Recall also that, by assumption, revocation succeeds with overwhelming probability, i.e.,

\Pr\left[{\sf IneffRevoke}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\sigma,\rho_{\textsc{R}})=\top\,:\,\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{A}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}\\
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)\\
\rho_{\textsc{R},\mathbf{\textsc{Aux}}}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}|\otimes\nu_{\lambda})\end{subarray}\right]=1-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

Using Bonferroni’s inequality, we can argue that

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\begin{subarray}{c}{\sf IneffRevoke}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\sigma,\textsc{R})=\top\vskip 0.56905pt\\
\bigwedge\vskip 0.56905pt\\
\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\textsc{Aux})\,\,\in\,\,\Lambda_{q}^{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{A})\,\cap\,\mathcal{B}^{m}(\mathbf{0},\sigma\sqrt{\frac{m}{2}})\end{subarray}\,:\,\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{A}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}\\
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)\\
\rho_{\textsc{R},\mathbf{\textsc{Aux}}}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}|\otimes\nu_{\lambda})\end{subarray}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\Pr\left[{\sf IneffRevoke}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\sigma,\rho_{\textsc{R}})=\top\,:\,\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{A}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}\\
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)\\
\rho_{\textsc{R},\mathbf{\textsc{Aux}}}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}|\otimes\nu_{\lambda})\end{subarray}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\Pr\left[\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\rho_{\textsc{Aux}})\,\,\in\,\,\Lambda_{q}^{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{A})\,\cap\,\mathcal{B}^{m}(\mathbf{0},\sigma\sqrt{m/2})\,:\,\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{A}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}\\
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)\\
\rho_{\textsc{R},\mathbf{\textsc{Aux}}}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}|\otimes\nu_{\lambda})\end{subarray}\right]-1

\displaystyle\geq\Pr\left[\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\rho_{\textsc{Aux}})\,\,\in\,\,\Lambda_{q}^{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{A})\,\cap\,\mathcal{B}^{m}(\mathbf{0},\sigma\sqrt{m/2})\,:\,\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{A}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}\\
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)\\
\rho_{\textsc{R},\mathbf{\textsc{Aux}}}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}|\otimes\nu_{\lambda})\end{subarray}\right]-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)

\displaystyle\geq\mathrm{poly}(\varepsilon,1/q).

This proves the claim.

∎

6.4
Distinct Pair Extraction

The following lemma allows us to analyze the probability of simultaneously extracting two distinct preimages in terms of the success probability of revocation and the success probability of extracting a preimage from the adversary’s state.

Lemma 6.20

(Distinct pair extraction)
.

Let
\rho\in\mathcal{D}({\mathcal{H}}_{X}\otimes{\mathcal{H}}_{Y})
be an any density matrix, for some Hilbert spaces
{\mathcal{H}}_{X}
and
{\mathcal{H}}_{Y}
. Let
\ket{\psi}=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{S}}\alpha_{x}\ket{x}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{X}
be any state supported on a subset
{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
, and let
\mathbf{\Pi}=\ket{\psi}\!\bra{\psi}
denote its associated projection.
Let
\mathbf{\Pi}_{\mathcal{S}}
be the projector onto
\mathcal{S}
with

\mathbf{\Pi}_{\mathcal{S}}=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{S}}|x\rangle\langle x|.

Let
\mathcal{E}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{Y})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{X^{\prime}})
be any
\mathsf{CPTP}
map of the form

\mathcal{E}_{Y\rightarrow X^{\prime}}(\sigma)=\mathrm{Tr}_{E}\left[V_{Y\rightarrow X^{\prime}E}\,\sigma V_{Y\rightarrow X^{\prime}E}^{\dagger}\right],\quad\forall\sigma\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{Y}),

for some isometry
V_{Y\rightarrow X^{\prime}E}
. Consider the measurement specified by

\mathbf{\Gamma}=\sum_{x,x^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}:x\neq x^{\prime}}\ket{x}\!\bra{x}_{X}\otimes V_{Y\rightarrow X^{\prime}E}^{\dagger}(\ket{x^{\prime}}\!\bra{x^{\prime}}_{X^{\prime}}\otimes I_{E})V_{Y\rightarrow X^{\prime}E}.

Let
\rho_{X}=\mathrm{Tr}_{Y}[\rho_{XY}]
denote the reduced state. Then, it holds that

\mathrm{Tr}[\mathbf{\Gamma}\rho]\,\geq\,\left(1-\max_{x\in{\mathcal{S}}}|\alpha_{x}|^{2}\right)\cdot\mathrm{Tr}[\mathbf{\Pi}\rho_{X}]\cdot\mathrm{Tr}\left[\mathbf{\Pi}_{\mathcal{S}}\,\mathcal{E}_{Y\rightarrow X^{\prime}}(\sigma)\right],

where
\sigma=\mathrm{Tr}[(\mathbf{\Pi}\otimes I)\rho]^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{Tr}_{X}[(\mathbf{\Pi}\otimes I)\rho]
is a reduced state in system
Y
.

Proof.

Because the order in which we apply
\mathbf{\Gamma}
and
(\mathbf{\Pi}\otimes I)
does not matter, we have the inequality

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\left[\mathbf{\Gamma}\rho\right]\geq\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\mathbf{\Pi}\otimes I\right)\mathbf{\Gamma}\rho\right]=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\left(\mathbf{\Pi}\otimes I\right)\mathbf{\Gamma}\rho\left(\mathbf{\Pi}\otimes I\right)\right]=\mathrm{Tr}\left[\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathbf{\Pi}\otimes I)\rho\left(\mathbf{\Pi}\otimes I\right)\right].

(1)

Notice also that
(\mathbf{\Pi}\otimes I)\rho(\mathbf{\Pi}\otimes I)
lies in the image of
(\mathbf{\Pi}\otimes I)
with
\mathbf{\Pi}=\ket{\psi}\!\bra{\psi}
, and thus

\displaystyle(\mathbf{\Pi}\otimes I)\rho(\mathbf{\Pi}\otimes I)=\mathrm{Tr}[(\mathbf{\Pi}\otimes I)\rho]\cdot(\ket{\psi}\!\bra{\psi}\otimes\sigma),

(2)

for some
\sigma\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{Y})
.
Putting everything together, we get that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\left[\mathbf{\Gamma}\rho\right]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\left[\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathbf{\Pi}\otimes I)\rho\left(\mathbf{\Pi}\otimes I\right)\right]\quad\quad\,\,\,\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{(using inequality \eqref{ineq:gamma})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}[(\mathbf{\Pi}\otimes I)\rho]\cdot\mathrm{Tr}\left[\mathbf{\Gamma}\left(\ket{\psi}\!\bra{\psi}\otimes\sigma\right)\right]\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\,\,\text{(using equation \eqref{ineq:sigma})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}[\mathbf{\Pi}\rho_{X}]\cdot\mathrm{Tr}\left[\sum_{x,x^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}:x\neq x^{\prime}}\ket{x}\!\bra{x}_{X}\otimes V_{Y\rightarrow X^{\prime}E}^{\dagger}\left(\ket{x^{\prime}}\!\bra{x^{\prime}}_{X^{\prime}}\otimes I_{E}\right)V_{Y\rightarrow X^{\prime}E}\left(\ket{\psi}\!\bra{\psi}\otimes\sigma\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}[\mathbf{\Pi}\rho_{X}]\cdot\sum_{x^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}}\left(\sum_{x\in{\mathcal{S}}:x\neq x^{\prime}}|\langle x|\psi\rangle|^{2}\right)\mathrm{Tr}\left[V_{Y\rightarrow X^{\prime}E}^{\dagger}(\ket{x^{\prime}}\!\bra{x^{\prime}}_{X^{\prime}}\otimes I_{E})V_{Y\rightarrow X^{\prime}E}\,\sigma\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}[\mathbf{\Pi}\rho_{X}]\cdot\sum_{x^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}}\left(1-|\alpha_{x^{\prime}}|^{2}\right)\mathrm{Tr}\left[(\ket{x^{\prime}}\!\bra{x^{\prime}}_{X^{\prime}}\otimes I_{E})V_{Y\rightarrow X^{\prime}E}\,\sigma\,V_{Y\rightarrow X^{\prime}E}^{\dagger}\right]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}[\mathbf{\Pi}\rho_{X}]\cdot\left(1-\max_{x\in{\mathcal{S}}}|\alpha_{x}|^{2}\right)\cdot\sum_{x^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[(\ket{x^{\prime}}\!\bra{x^{\prime}}_{X^{\prime}}\otimes I_{E})V_{Y\rightarrow X^{\prime}E}\,\sigma\,V_{Y\rightarrow X^{\prime}E}^{\dagger}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}[\mathbf{\Pi}\rho_{X}]\cdot\left(1-\max_{x\in{\mathcal{S}}}|\alpha_{x}|^{2}\right)\cdot\sum_{x^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\ket{x^{\prime}}\!\bra{x^{\prime}}_{X^{\prime}}\mathrm{Tr}_{E}\left[V_{Y\rightarrow X^{\prime}E}\,\sigma\,V_{Y\rightarrow X^{\prime}E}^{\dagger}\right]\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}[\mathbf{\Pi}\rho_{X}]\cdot\left(1-\max_{x\in{\mathcal{S}}}|\alpha_{x}|^{2}\right)\cdot\mathrm{Tr}\left[\mathbf{\Pi}_{\mathcal{S}}\,\mathcal{E}_{Y\rightarrow X^{\prime}}(\sigma)\right].

This proves the claim.
∎

6.5
Proof of Theorem
6.1

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
\mathsf{QPT}
adversary and suppose that

\left|\Pr\left[1\leftarrow\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda},0)\right]-\Pr\left[1\leftarrow\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda},1)\right]\right|=\varepsilon(\lambda),

for some
\varepsilon(\lambda)
with respect to
\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda},b)
in
Figure

13
. We show that
\varepsilon(\lambda)
is negligible.

\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda},b)
:

1.

The challenger samples
(\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m},\mathsf{td}_{\mathbf{A}})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenTrap}(1^{n},1^{m},q)
and generates

\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}\,\,=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}\\
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{y}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q)\end{subarray}}\rho_{\sigma}(\mathbf{x})\,\ket{\mathbf{x}},

for some
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
, by running
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)
. The challenger lets
\mathsf{MSK}\leftarrow\mathsf{td}_{\mathbf{A}}
and
\mathsf{PK}\leftarrow(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y})
and sends
\rho_{\mathsf{SK}}\leftarrow\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}
to the adversary
\mathcal{A}
.

2.

\mathcal{A}
generates a (possibly entangled) bipartite state
\rho_{R,\mathbf{\textsc{aux}}}
in systems
\mathcal{H}_{R}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\textsc{Aux}}
with
\mathcal{H}_{R}=\mathcal{H}_{q}^{m}
, returns system
R
and holds onto the auxiliary system
Aux
.

3.

The challenger runs
\mathsf{Revoke}(\mathsf{PK},\mathsf{MSK},\rho_{R})
, where
\rho_{R}
is the reduced state in system
R
. If the outcome is
\top
, the game continues. Otherwise, output
Invalid
.

4.

\mathcal{A}
submits a plaintext bit
\mu\in{\{0,1\}}
.

5.

The challenger does the following depending on
b\in{\{0,1\}}
:

•

if
b=0
: the challenger samples a vector
\mathbf{s}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
and errors
\mathbf{e}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z}^{m},\,\alpha q}
and
e^{\prime}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z},\,\beta q}
, and sends a Dual-Regev encryption of
\mu\in{\{0,1\}}
to
\mathcal{A}
:

\mathsf{CT}=\left(\mathbf{s}^{\intercal}\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{e}^{\intercal},\mathbf{s}^{\intercal}\mathbf{y}+e^{\prime}+\mu\cdot\lfloor\frac{q}{2}\rfloor\right)\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}\times\mathbb{Z}_{q}.

•

if
b=1
: the challenger samples
\mathbf{u}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
and
r\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}
uniformly at random and sends the following pair to
\mathcal{A}
:

(\mathbf{u},r)\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}\times\mathbb{Z}_{q}.

6.

\mathcal{A}
returns a bit
b^{\prime}\in{\{0,1\}}
.

Figure 13:
The key-revocable security experiment according to
Definition

5.3
.

Suppose for the sake of contradiction that
\epsilon(\lambda)
is non-negligible. Using the equivalence between prediction advantage and distinguishing advantage, we can write

2\cdot\left|\Pr\left[b\leftarrow\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda},b)\,:\,b\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}{\{0,1\}}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\right|=\varepsilon(\lambda).

We show that we can use
\mathcal{A}
to break the
\mathsf{SIS}_{n,q,\sigma\sqrt{2m}}^{m}
problem. Without loss of generality, we assume that
\mathcal{A}
submits the plaintext
x=0
. By the assumption that revocation succeeds with overwhelming probability and since
\epsilon(\lambda)\geq 1/\mathrm{poly}(\lambda)
, we can use
Theorem

6.19
to argue that
there exists a quantum Goldreich-Levin extractor
\mathcal{E}
that takes as input
\mathbf{A}
,
\mathbf{y}
and system
Aux
of the state
\rho_{R,\mathbf{\textsc{Aux}}}
and outputs a short vector in the coset
\Lambda_{q}^{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{A})
in time
\mathrm{poly}(\lambda,m,\sigma,q,1/\varepsilon)
such that

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\begin{subarray}{c}{\sf IneffRevoke}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\sigma,\textsc{R})=\top\vskip 0.56905pt\\
\bigwedge\vskip 0.56905pt\\
\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\textsc{Aux})\,\,\in\,\,\Lambda_{q}^{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{A})\,\cap\,\mathcal{B}^{m}(\mathbf{0},\sigma\sqrt{\frac{m}{2}})\end{subarray}\,:\,\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{A}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}\\
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)\\
\rho_{R,\mathbf{\textsc{Aux}}}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}|)\end{subarray}\right]\,\geq\,\mathrm{poly}(\varepsilon,1/q).

Here, we rely on the correctness of
\mathsf{GenTrap}
in
Theorem

2.13
and
\mathsf{QSampGauss}
in
Theorem

3.3
.

Consider the following procedure in Algorithm
3
.

Input:

Matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
.

Output:

Vector
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
.

1

Generate a Gaussian state
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)
with

\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}\,\,=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}\\
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{y}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q)\end{subarray}}\rho_{\sigma}(\mathbf{x})\,\ket{\mathbf{x}}

for some vector
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
.

2

3

Run
\mathcal{A}
to generate a bipartite state
\rho_{R\,\mathbf{\textsc{Aux}}}
in systems
\mathcal{H}_{\textsc{R}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\textsc{aux}}
with
\mathcal{H}_{\textsc{R}}=\mathcal{H}_{q}^{m}
.

4

5

Measure system
R
in the computational basis, and let
\mathbf{x}_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
denote the outcome.

6

7

Run the quantum Goldreich-Levin extractor
\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\rho_{\mathbf{\textsc{aux}}})
from
Theorem

6.16
, where
\rho_{\mathbf{\textsc{Aux}}}
is the reduced state in system
\mathcal{H}_{\textsc{Aux}}
, and let
\mathbf{x}_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
denote the outcome.

8

9

Output the vector
\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{0}
.

Algorithm 3

\mathsf{SIS\_Solver}(\mathbf{A})

To conclude the proof, we show that
\mathsf{SIS\_Solver}(\mathbf{A})
in Algorithm
3
breaks the
\mathsf{SIS}_{n,q,\sigma\sqrt{2m}}^{m}
problem whenever
\varepsilon(\lambda)=1/\mathrm{poly}(\lambda)
. In order to guarantee that
\mathsf{SIS\_Solver}(\mathbf{A})
is successful, we use the distinct pair extraction result of
Lemma

6.20
. This allows us to analyze the probability of simultaneously extracting two distinct short pre-images
\mathbf{x}_{0}\neq\mathbf{x}_{1}
such that
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{0}=\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{1}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q)
– both in terms of the success probability of revocation and the success probability of extracting a pre-image from the adversary’s state
\rho_{\mathbf{\textsc{Aux}}}
in system
\mathcal{H}_{\textsc{Aux}}
. Assuming that
\mathbf{x}_{0},\mathbf{x}_{1}
are distinct short pre-images such that
\|\mathbf{x}_{0}\|\leq\sigma\sqrt{\frac{m}{2}}
and
\|\mathbf{x}_{1}\|\leq\sigma\sqrt{\frac{m}{2}}
, it then follows that the vector
\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{0}
output by
\mathsf{SIS\_Solver}(\mathbf{A})
has norm at most
\sigma\sqrt{2m}
, and thus yields a solution to
\mathsf{SIS}_{n,q,\sigma\sqrt{2m}}^{m}
.

We remark that the state
\ket{\psi_{{\mathbf{y}}}}
prepared by
Algorithm
3
is not normalized for ease of notation. Note that the tail bound in
Lemma

2.6
implies that (the normalized variant of)
\ket{\psi_{{\mathbf{y}}}}
is within negligible trace distance of the state
with support
\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}:\|\mathbf{x}\|\leq\sigma\sqrt{\frac{m}{2}}\}
. Therefore, for the sake of
Lemma

6.20
, we can assume that
\ket{\psi_{{\mathbf{y}}}}
is a normalized state of the form

\ket{\psi_{{\mathbf{y}}}}=\left(\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m},\|\mathbf{z}\|\leq\sigma\sqrt{\frac{m}{2}}\\
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{z}=\mathbf{y}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q)\end{subarray}}\rho_{\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{2}}}(\mathbf{z})\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m},\|\mathbf{x}\|\leq\sigma\sqrt{\frac{m}{2}}\\
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{y}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q)\end{subarray}}\rho_{\sigma}(\mathbf{x})\ket{\mathbf{x}}.

Before we analyze Algorithm
3
, we first make two technical remarks. First, since
\sigma\geq\omega(\sqrt{\log m})
, it follows from
Lemma

2.10

that, for any full-rank
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
and for any
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
, we have

\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m},\,\|\mathbf{x}\|\leq\sigma\sqrt{\frac{m}{2}}\\
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{y}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q)\end{subarray}}\left\{\frac{\rho_{\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{2}}}(\mathbf{x})}{\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m},\|\mathbf{z}\|\leq\sigma\sqrt{\frac{m}{2}}\\
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{z}=\mathbf{y}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q)\end{subarray}}\rho_{\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{2}}}(\mathbf{z})}\right\}\,\,\leq\,\,2^{-\Omega(m)}.

Second, we can replace the procedure
\mathsf{Revoke}(\mathbf{A},\mathsf{td}_{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{y},\rho_{R})

by an (inefficient) projective measurement
\{|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}|,I-|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}|\}
, since they produce statistically close outcomes. This follows from the fact that

\mathsf{Revoke}(\mathbf{A},\mathsf{td}_{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{y},\rho_{\textsc{R}})
applies the procedure
\mathsf{QSampGauss}
in Algorithm
2
as a subroutine, which is correct with overwhelming probability acccording to
Theorem

3.3
.

Let us now analyze the success probability of Algorithm
3
. Putting everything together, we get

\displaystyle{\sf Pr}\left[\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\leftarrow{\sf SIS}\_{\sf Solver}(\mathbf{A})\vskip 2.84526pt\\
\bigwedge\vskip 1.42262pt\\
\mathbf{x}\neq\mathbf{0}\,\text{ s.t.}\,\,\|\mathbf{x}\|\leq\sigma\sqrt{2m}\end{subarray}\ \,:\,\ \mathbf{A}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\left(1-\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m},\,\|\mathbf{x}\|\leq\sigma\sqrt{\frac{m}{2}}\\
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{y}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q)\end{subarray}}\left\{\frac{\rho_{\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{2}}}(\mathbf{x})}{\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m},\|\mathbf{z}\|\leq\sigma\sqrt{\frac{m}{2}}\\
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{z}=\mathbf{y}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q)\end{subarray}}\rho_{\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{2}}}(\mathbf{z})}\right\}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\cdot\Pr\left[{\sf IneffRevoke}({\mathbf{A}},{\mathbf{y}},\rho_{R})=\top\,\,:\,\,\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{A}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}\text{ s.t. }{\mathbf{A}}\text{ is full-rank}\\
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)\\
\rho_{\textsc{R},\mathbf{\textsc{Aux}}}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}|)\end{subarray}\right]

\displaystyle\quad\cdot\Pr\left[\mathcal{E}\big(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\rho_{\mathbf{\textsc{Aux}}}\big)\,\,\in\,\,\Lambda_{q}^{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{A})\cap\mathcal{B}^{m}(\mathbf{0},\sigma\sqrt{m/2})\,\,:\,\,\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{A}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}\text{ s.t. }{\mathbf{A}}\text{ is full-rank}\\
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)\\
\rho_{\textsc{R},\mathbf{\textsc{Aux}}}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}|)\\
\top\leftarrow{\sf IneffRevoke}({\mathbf{A}},{\mathbf{y}},\rho_{\textsc{R}})\end{subarray}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\left(1-2^{-\Omega(m)}\right)\cdot\Pr\left[\begin{subarray}{c}{\sf IneffRevoke}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\sigma,\textsc{R})=\top\vskip 0.56905pt\\
\bigwedge\vskip 0.56905pt\\
\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\textsc{Aux})\,\,\in\,\,\Lambda_{q}^{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{A})\,\cap\,\mathcal{B}^{m}(\mathbf{0},\sigma\sqrt{\frac{m}{2}})\end{subarray}\,:\,\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{A}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}\text{ s.t. }{\mathbf{A}}\text{ is full-rank}\\
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)\\
\rho_{\textsc{R},\mathbf{\textsc{Aux}}}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}|)\end{subarray}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\left(1-2^{-\Omega(m)}\right)\cdot\big(\mathrm{poly}(\varepsilon,1/q)-q^{-n}\big)\quad\geq\quad\mathrm{poly}(\varepsilon,1/q).

In the last line, we applied the simultaneous search-to-decision reduction from
Theorem

6.19
and
Lemma

2.4
.
Therefore,
\mathsf{SIS\_Solver}(\mathbf{A})
in Algorithm
3
runs in time
\mathrm{poly}(q,1/\varepsilon)
and solves
\mathsf{SIS}_{n,q,\sigma\sqrt{2m}}^{m}
whenever
\varepsilon=1/\mathrm{poly}(\lambda)
. Therefore, we conclude that
\varepsilon(\lambda)
must be negligible.
∎

6.6
Proof of Theorem
6.2

Proof.

The proof is the same as in
Theorem

6.1
, except that we invoke

Theorem

6.16
instead of
Theorem

6.19
to argue that simultaneous extraction succeeds with sufficiently high probability.
∎

7
Key-Revocable Dual-Regev Encryption with Classical Revocation

Recall that our key-revocable Dual-Regev public-key encryption scheme in
1
requires that a quantum state is
returned
as part of revocation. In this section, we present a Dual-Regev public-key encryption scheme with
classical key revocation
(
2
).

To prove security, we follow a similar proof as in our previous construction from
Section

6
. As an additional technical ingredient, we rely on a strong type of
collapsing
property Ajtai hash recently proven by Bartusek, Khurana and Poremba
[
BKP23
, Theorem 5.5]
, which relies on the hardness of subexponential
\mathsf{LWE}
and
\mathsf{SIS}
.

7.1
Definition: Public-Key Encryption with Classical Key Revocation

Let us now give a formal definition of a public-key encryption scheme with classical key revocation.

Definition 7.1

(Key-Revocable Public-Key Encryption with Classical Revocation)
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
be the security parameter. A key-revocable public-key encryption scheme with classical revocation consists efficient algorithms
\left(\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec},\mathsf{Delete},\mathsf{Revoke}\right)
, where
\mathsf{Enc}
and
\mathsf{Revoke}
are
\mathsf{PPT}
algorithms, and
\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Dec}
and
\mathsf{Delete}
are
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithms defined as follows:

•

\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})
: given as input a security parameter
\lambda
, output a public key
\mathsf{PK}
, a master secret key
\mathsf{MSK}
and a quantum decryption key
\rho_{\mathsf{SK}}
.

•

\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{PK},x)
: given a public key
\mathsf{PK}
and plaintext
x\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
, output a ciphertext
\mathsf{CT}
.

•

\mathsf{Dec}(\rho_{\mathsf{SK}},\mathsf{CT})
: given a decryption key
\rho_{\mathsf{SK}}
and ciphertext
\mathsf{CT}
, output a message
y
.

•

\mathsf{Delete}(\rho_{\mathsf{SK}})
: given a quantum decryption key, it outputs a classical certificate
\pi
.

•

\mathsf{Revoke}\left(\mathsf{PK},\mathsf{MSK},\pi\right)
: given as input a master secret key
\mathsf{MSK}
, a public key
\mathsf{PK}
and a certificate
\pi
, output
\mathsf{Valid}
or
\mathsf{Invalid}
.

Correctness of Decryption.

For every
x\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
, the following holds:

{\sf Pr}\left[x\leftarrow\mathsf{Dec}(\rho_{\mathsf{SK}},\mathsf{CT})\ :\ \begin{subarray}{c}(\mathsf{PK},\mathsf{MSK},\rho_{\mathsf{SK}})\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})\\
\ \\
\mathsf{CT}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{PK},x)\end{subarray}\right]\geq 1-\nu(\lambda),

where
\nu(\cdot)
is a negligible function.

Correctness of Revocation.

The following holds:

{\sf Pr}\left[\mathsf{Valid}\leftarrow\mathsf{Revoke}\left(\mathsf{PK},\mathsf{MSK},\pi\right)\ :\ \begin{subarray}{c}(\mathsf{PK},\mathsf{MSK},\rho_{\mathsf{SK}})\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})\\
\pi\leftarrow\mathsf{Delete}(\rho_{\mathsf{SK}})\end{subarray}\right]\geq 1-\nu(\lambda),

where
\nu(\cdot)
is a negligible function.

Our security definition for key-revocable public-key encryption is as follows.

\mathsf{Expt}_{\Sigma,\mathcal{A}}\left(1^{\lambda},b\right)
:

Initialization Phase
:

•

The challenger runs
(\mathsf{PK},\mathsf{MSK},\rho_{\mathsf{SK}})\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})
and sends
(\mathsf{PK},\rho_{\mathsf{SK}})
to
\mathcal{A}
.

Revocation Phase
:

•

The challenger sends the message
REVOKE
to
\mathcal{A}
.

•

The adversary
\mathcal{A}
returns a certificate
\pi
.

•

The challenger aborts if
\mathsf{Revoke}(\mathsf{PK},\mathsf{MSK},\pi)
outputs
\mathsf{Invalid}
.

Guessing Phase
:

•

\mathcal{A}
submits a plaintext
x\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
to the challenger.

•

If
b=0
: The challenger sends
\mathsf{CT}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{PK},x)
to
\mathcal{A}
. Else, if
b=1
, the challenger sends
\mathsf{CT}\xleftarrow{\$}{\mathcal{C}}
, where
{\mathcal{C}}
is the ciphertext space of
\ell
bit messages.

•

Output
b_{\mathcal{A}}
if the output of
\mathcal{A}
is
b_{\mathcal{A}}
.

Figure 14:
Security Experiment for Classical Key Revocation

Definition 7.2
.

A public-key encryption scheme
\Sigma=\left(\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec},\mathsf{Delete},\mathsf{Revoke}\right)
with classical key revocation is
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-secure if, for every
\mathsf{QPT}
adversary
\mathcal{A}
with

{\sf Pr}[\mathsf{Invalid}\leftarrow\mathsf{Expt}_{\Sigma,\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda},b)]\leq\delta(\lambda)

for
b\in{\{0,1\}}
, it holds that

\left|\Pr\left[1\leftarrow\mathsf{Expt}_{\Sigma,\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda},0)\right]-\Pr\left[1\leftarrow\mathsf{Expt}_{\Sigma,\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda},1)\right]\right|\leq\varepsilon(\lambda),

where
\mathsf{Expt}_{\Sigma,\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda},b)
is as defined in
Figure

14
. If
\delta(\lambda)=1-1/\mathrm{poly}(\lambda)
and
\varepsilon(\lambda)=\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
, we simply say the key-revocable public-key encryption scheme is secure.

7.2
Construction.

Preliminaries.

Let us first introduce some additional background on lattice trapdoors.

Definition 7.3

(
\beta
-good trapdoor)
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
be a matrix. A
\beta
-good lattice trapdoor
\mathbf{T}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m\times m}
for the matrix
\mathbf{A}
is a short basis for the lattice
\Lambda_{q}^{\perp}(\mathbf{A})
with the following properties:

1.

Each column vector of
\mathbf{T}
is in the right kernel of
\mathbf{A}
(mod
q
), i.e.
\mathbf{A}\cdot\mathbf{T}=\mathbf{0}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q)
.

2.

Each column vector of
\mathbf{T}=[\mathbf{t}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{t}_{m}]
is short, i.e.
\|\mathbf{t}_{i}\|\leq\beta
, for all
i\in[m]
.

3.

The matrix
\mathbf{T}
has full rank
m
over
\mathbb{R}
.

We make use of the following specific variant of a lattice trapdoor for the
q
-ary lattice
\Lambda_{q}^{\perp}(\mathbf{A})
due to Alwen and Peikert
[
AP08
]
.

Lemma 7.4

(AP lattice trapdoor,
[
AP08
]
, Lemma 3.5)
.

Let
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
and
q\in\mathbb{N}
be a prime such that
m=\lceil 6n\log q\rceil
. There exists a randomized algorithm with the following properties:

•

\mathsf{GenTrap}(1^{n},1^{m},q)
: on input
1^{n},1^{m}
and
q
, returns a matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
and a short trapdoor matrix
\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m\times m}
such that the distribution of
\mathbf{A}
is negligibly (in the parameter
n
) close to uniform and
\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}}
is a
(20n\log q)
-good lattice trapdoor with all but negligible probability.

Let us now present our Dual-Regev encryption scheme with classical key revocation

Parameters.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
be the security parameter. Let
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
and
q\in\mathbb{N}
be a prime with
q=2^{o(n)}
such that
m=\lceil 6n\log q\rceil
and
\nu=64m^{2}
, each parameterized by
\lambda
. Choose a parameter
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\sqrt{8m}<\sigma<q/\sqrt{8m}
and
\sigma/\nu=2^{o(n)}
. Moreover, let
\alpha,\beta\in(0,1)
be noise ratios with the property that
\beta/\alpha=2^{o(n)}
such that
1/\alpha=2^{o(n)}\cdot\sigma
.

Construction 2

(Dual-Regev Encryption with Classical Key Revocation)
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
be the security parameter. The key-revocable scheme
\mathsf{CRevDual}=(\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec},\mathsf{Delete},\mathsf{Revoke})
with classical revocation consists of the following
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithms:

•

\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})\rightarrow(\mathsf{PK},\rho_{\mathsf{SK}},\mathsf{MSK}):
sample
(\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m},\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m\times m})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenTrap}(1^{n},1^{m},q)
using the procedure in
Lemma

7.4
, sample a random vector
\mathbf{v}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}{\{0,1\}}^{m}
, generate a Gaussian superposition
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)
, and let

\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}}=\mathbf{Z}_{q}^{\lfloor\frac{q}{\nu}\rfloor\cdot\mathbf{v}}\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}\\
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{y}\end{subarray}}\rho_{\sigma}(\mathbf{x})\,\omega_{q}^{\langle\mathbf{x},\lfloor\frac{q}{\nu}\rfloor\cdot\mathbf{v}\rangle}\,\ket{\mathbf{x}}.

Output
\mathsf{PK}=(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y})
,
\rho_{\mathsf{SK}}=\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}}
and
\mathsf{MSK}=(\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{v})
.

•

\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{PK},\mu)\rightarrow\mathsf{CT}:
to encrypt a bit
\mu\in{\{0,1\}}
, sample a random vector
\mathbf{s}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
and errors
\mathbf{e}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z}^{m},\,\alpha q}
and
e^{\prime}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z},\,\beta q}
, and output the ciphertext pair

\mathsf{CT}=\left(\mathbf{s}^{\intercal}\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{e}^{\intercal}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q),\mathbf{s}^{\intercal}\mathbf{y}+e^{\prime}+\mu\cdot\lfloor\frac{q}{2}\rfloor\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q)\right)\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}\times\mathbb{Z}_{q}.

•

\mathsf{Dec}(\rho_{\mathsf{SK}},\mathsf{CT})\rightarrow{\{0,1\}}:
to decrypt
\mathsf{CT}
,
apply the unitary
U:\ket{\mathbf{x}}\ket{0}\rightarrow\ket{\mathbf{x}}\ket{\mathsf{CT}\cdot(-\mathbf{x},1)^{\intercal}}
on input
\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}}\ket{0}
, where
\rho_{\mathsf{SK}}=\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}}
, and measure the second register in the computational basis. Output
0
, if the measurement outcome
is closer to
0
than to
\lfloor\frac{q}{2}\rfloor
,
and output
1
, otherwise.
14
14

14

This procedure can be purified via the Gentle Measurement Lemma.

•

\mathsf{Delete}(\rho)\rightarrow\mathbf{w}
: apply the
m
-qudit
q
-ary quantum Fourier transform to the state
\rho
, and measure in the computational basis to obtain an outcome
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
.

•

\mathsf{Revoke}(\mathsf{MSK},\mathbf{w})\rightarrow\{\top,\bot\}
: to verify the certificate
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
, do the following:

1.

Parse
(\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m\times m},\mathbf{v}\in{\{0,1\}}^{m})\leftarrow\mathsf{MSK}
.

2.

Compute
\mathbf{c}^{\intercal}=\mathbf{w}^{\intercal}\cdot\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q)
.

3.

Compute
\mathbf{d}^{\intercal}=\mathbf{c}^{\intercal}\cdot\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}}^{-1}
, where
\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}}^{-1}
is the inverse matrix of
\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}}
over
\mathbb{R}
.
15
15

15

The matrix
\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m\times m}
output by
\mathsf{GenTrap}(1^{n},1^{m},q)
has full rank
m
over
\mathbb{R}
with overwhelming probability.

4.

For
i\in[m]
, let
v^{\prime}_{i}=0
, if
d_{i}\in[-q/\sigma,q/\sigma]
, and let
v^{\prime}_{i}=1
, otherwise.

5.

Output
\top
, if
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}=\mathbf{v}
, and output
\bot
otherwise.

Correctness of verification.

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
be the security parameter. Then, by our choice of parameters.

{\sf Pr}\left[\top\leftarrow\mathsf{Revoke}(\mathsf{MSK},\mathbf{w})\ :\ \begin{subarray}{c}(\mathsf{PK},\mathsf{MSK},\rho_{\mathsf{SK}})\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})\\
\ \\
\mathbf{w}\leftarrow\mathsf{Delete}(\rho_{\mathsf{SK}})\end{subarray}\right]\geq 1-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)

Proof.

Recall that
\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})
samples
(\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m},\mathbf{T}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m\times m})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenTrap}(1^{n},1^{m},q)
and
\mathbf{v}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}{\{0,1\}}^{m}
, generates a Gaussian superposition
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)
and assigns
\mathsf{PK}=(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y})
,
\rho_{\mathsf{SK}}=\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}}
and
\mathsf{MSK}=(\mathbf{T},\mathbf{v})
, where

\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}}=\mathbf{Z}_{q}^{\lfloor\frac{q}{\nu}\rfloor\cdot\mathbf{v}}\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}\\
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{y}\end{subarray}}\rho_{\sigma}(\mathbf{x})\,\omega_{q}^{\langle\mathbf{x},\lfloor\frac{q}{\nu}\rfloor\cdot\mathbf{v}\rangle}\,\ket{\mathbf{x}}.

From
Lemma

7.4
and
Lemma

2.4
, it follows that
\mathbf{A}
is full rank and that
\mathbf{T}
is a
(20n\log q)
-good lattice trapdoor with overwhelming probability.
Moreover, from
Lemma

3.2
, it follows that
\mathbf{w}\leftarrow\mathsf{Del}(\rho_{\mathsf{SK}})
results in a vector
\mathbf{w}^{\intercal}=\hat{\mathbf{s}}^{\intercal}\mathbf{A}+\lfloor\frac{q}{\nu}\rfloor\cdot\mathbf{v}^{\intercal}+\hat{\mathbf{e}}^{\intercal}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
with
\hat{\mathbf{e}}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m},q/\sigma}
. Therefore, we have that with overwhelming probability
\|\hat{\mathbf{e}}\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{m}q/\sigma
and thus

\displaystyle\|\lfloor\frac{q}{\nu}\rfloor\cdot\mathbf{v}^{\intercal}\cdot\mathbf{T}+\hat{\mathbf{e}}^{\intercal}\cdot\mathbf{T}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\lfloor\frac{q}{\nu}\rfloor\cdot\|\mathbf{v}^{\intercal}\mathbf{T}\|_{2}+\|\hat{\mathbf{e}}^{\intercal}\mathbf{T}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\lfloor\frac{q}{\nu}\rfloor\cdot\|\mathbf{v}\|_{2}\cdot\|\mathbf{T}\|_{2}+\|\hat{\mathbf{e}}\|_{2}\cdot\|\mathbf{T}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\lfloor\frac{q}{\nu}\rfloor\cdot\sqrt{m}\cdot\|\mathbf{T}\|_{2}+\sqrt{m}\cdot\frac{q}{\sigma}\cdot\|\mathbf{T}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\lfloor\frac{q}{\nu}\rfloor+\frac{q}{\sigma}\right)\cdot m\cdot\|\mathbf{T}\|\,\leq\,q/4.

Consequently,
\mathbf{c}^{\intercal}=\mathbf{w}^{\intercal}\cdot\mathbf{T}=\lfloor\frac{q}{\nu}\rfloor\cdot\mathbf{v}^{\intercal}\cdot\mathbf{T}+\hat{\mathbf{e}}^{\intercal}\cdot\mathbf{T}\,\in\,\mathbb{Z}^{m}\cap(-\frac{q}{4},\frac{q}{4}]^{m}
and thus

\mathbf{d}^{\intercal}=\mathbf{c}^{\intercal}\cdot\mathbf{T}^{-1}=\lfloor\frac{q}{\nu}\rfloor\cdot\mathbf{v}^{\intercal}+\hat{\mathbf{e}}^{\intercal}.

By our choice of parameters, we have that
\lfloor\frac{q}{\nu}\rfloor\gg\frac{q}{\sigma}
, and thus the procedure
\mathsf{Revoke}(\mathsf{MSK},\mathbf{w})
successfully decodes
\mathbf{v}
from
\mathbf{d}
, and outputs
\top
with overwhelming probability.

∎

7.3
Proof of Security.

We first discuss some relevant background which we use for the security proof.

Everlasting collapsing property of the Ajtai hash function.

We need the following result from Bartusek, Khurana and Poremba
[
BKP23
, Theorem 5.5]
. Informally, it says that once the adversary outputs a short pre-image (given as input either a Gaussian superposition of pre-images, or a single measured pre-image), then the adversary cannot tell which state it initially received even if it is allowed to be unbounded for the rest of the experiment. This property is called
certified-everlasting target-collapsing
16
16

16

It’s called “everlasting” because the adversary can be unbounded once a pre-image is presented and it’s called “target-collapsing” because it is a weakening of collapsing, where the image is honestly generated by the challenger.
and it can be shown assuming the hardness of
\mathsf{LWE}
/
\mathsf{SIS}
.

Theorem 7.6

(Certified-everlasting target-collapsing property of the Ajtai hash,
[
BKP23
]
)
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
q
be a prime modulus with
q=2^{o(n)}
and
m\geq 2n\log q
, each parameterized by the security parameter
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
q/\sqrt{8m}>\sigma>\sqrt{8m}
and let
\alpha\in(0,1)
be a noise ratio such that
1/\alpha=2^{o(n)}\cdot\sigma
.
Then, assuming the hardness of the
\mathsf{LWE}_{n,q,\alpha q}^{m}
and
\mathsf{SIS}_{n,q,\sigma\sqrt{2m}}^{m}
, it holds for any adversary

\mathcal{A}=(\mathcal{A}_{0},\mathcal{A}_{1})
consisting of a
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithm
\mathcal{A}_{0}
and an unbounded algorithm
\mathcal{A}_{1}
:

|\Pr\left[\mathsf{EvTargetCollapseExpt}_{\mathcal{A},\lambda}(0)=1\right]-\Pr\left[\mathsf{EvTargetCollapseExpt}_{\mathcal{A},\lambda}(1)=1\right]|\leq\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

Here, the experiment
\mathsf{EvTargetCollapseExpt}_{\mathcal{A},\lambda}(b)
is defined as follows:

1.

The challenger generates a Gaussian superposition
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)
, with

\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}\\
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{y}\end{subarray}}\rho_{\sigma}(\mathbf{x})\,\ket{\mathbf{x}}.

2.

If
b=0
, the challenger does nothing. Else, if
b=1
, the challenger measures the state in the computational basis. Next, the challenger sends the resulting state together with
y
to
\mathcal{A}_{0}
.

3.

\mathcal{A}_{0}
sends a classical certificate
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
to the challenger and initializes
\mathcal{A}_{1}
with its residual (internal) state.

4.

The challenger checks if
\mathbf{w}
satisfies
\mathbf{A}\cdot\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{y}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q)
and
\|\mathbf{w}\|\leq\sigma\sqrt{m/2}
. If true,
\mathcal{A}_{1}
is run until it outputs a bit
b^{\prime}
. Otherwise,
b^{\prime}\leftarrow\{0,1\}
is sampled uniformly at random. The output of the experiment is
b^{\prime}
.

We immediately obtain result.

Theorem 7.7

(Uncertainty principle for Gaussian states)
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
q
be a prime modulus with
q=2^{o(n)}
and
m\geq 2n\log q
, each parameterized by the security parameter
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
q/\sqrt{8m}>\sigma>\sqrt{8m}
and let
\alpha\in(0,1)
be a noise ratio such that
1/\alpha=2^{o(n)}\cdot\sigma
, and let
\nu>0
.
Then, assuming the subexponential hardness of the
\mathsf{LWE}_{n,q,\alpha q}^{m}
, it holds for any adversary

\mathcal{A}=(\mathcal{A}_{0},\mathcal{A}_{1})
consisting of a
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithm
\mathcal{A}_{0}
and an unbounded algorithm
\mathcal{A}_{1}
:

\Pr[\mathsf{UncertaintyExpt}_{\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda})=1]\leq\mathsf{negl}(\lambda),

where
\mathsf{UncertaintyExpt}_{\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda})
is the following experiment between a challenger and the adversary
\mathcal{A}
:

1.

The challenger samples a random matrix
\mathbf{A}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
and vector
\mathbf{v}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}{\{0,1\}}^{m}
, generates a Gaussian superposition
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)
and prepares

\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}}=\mathbf{Z}_{q}^{\lfloor\frac{q}{\nu}\rfloor\cdot\mathbf{v}}\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}\\
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{y}\end{subarray}}\rho_{\sigma}(\mathbf{x})\,\omega_{q}^{\langle\mathbf{x},\lfloor\frac{q}{\nu}\rfloor\cdot\mathbf{v}\rangle}\,\ket{\mathbf{x}}.

Then, the challenger sends
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}})
to
\mathcal{A}_{0}
.

2.

\mathcal{A}_{0}
generates a vector
\mathbf{x}_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
and a polynomial-sized quantum state
\rho_{\textsc{Aux}}
. Then,
\mathcal{A}_{0}
sends
\mathbf{x}_{0}
to the challenger and forwards
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\rho_{\textsc{Aux}})
to
\mathcal{A}_{1}
.

3.

\mathcal{A}_{1}
generates a vector
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
on input
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\rho_{\textsc{Aux}})
and sends it to the challenger.

4.

The challenger checks if
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}=\mathbf{v}
and whether the vector
\mathbf{x}_{0}
satisfies
\mathbf{A}\cdot\mathbf{x}_{0}=\mathbf{y}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q)
and
\|\mathbf{x}_{0}\|\leq\sigma\sqrt{m/2}
. If true, the challenger outputs
1
, otherwise the challenger outputs
0
. The bit output by the challenger also denotes the outcome of the experiment.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{A}=(\mathcal{A}_{0},\mathcal{A}_{1})
be an adversary consisting of a
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithm
\mathcal{A}_{0}
and an unbounded algorithm
\mathcal{A}_{1}
. We consider the following two hybrids:

\mathsf{H}_{0}
: This is the original experiment
\mathsf{UncertaintyExpt}_{\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda})
.

\mathsf{H}_{1}
: This is the same experiment as before, except that the challenger additionally measures the state
\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}}
in Step
1
before sending it to
\mathcal{A}_{0}
.

To complete the proof, we show the following:

Claim 7.8
.

|\Pr\left[\mathsf{H}_{0}=1\right]-\Pr\left[\mathsf{H}_{1}=1\right]|\leq\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

Proof.

This follows from the certified-everlasting target-collapsing property of the Ajtai hash in
Theorem

7.6
. Suppose for the sake of contradiction that the advantage is
1/\mathrm{poly}(\lambda)
. We consider the following adversary
\mathcal{B}=(\mathcal{B}_{0},\mathcal{B}_{1})
consisting of a
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithm
\mathcal{B}_{0}
and an unbounded algorithm
\mathcal{B}_{1}
that distinguishes
\mathsf{EvTargetCollapseExpt}_{\mathcal{B},\lambda}(1^{\lambda},b)
for
b\in{\{0,1\}}
with advantage
1/\mathrm{poly}(\lambda)
.

•

\mathcal{B}_{0}
receives as input
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}})
from the challenger, samples
\mathbf{v}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}{\{0,1\}}^{m}
, generates the state

\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}}=\mathbf{Z}_{q}^{\lfloor\frac{q}{\nu}\rfloor\cdot\mathbf{v}}\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}

and then forwards
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}})
to
\mathcal{A}_{0}
.

•

When
\mathcal{A}_{0}
outputs a vector
\mathbf{x}_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
and a polynomial-size advice state
\rho_{\textsc{Aux}}
,
\mathcal{B}_{0}
forwards
\mathbf{x}_{0}
to the challenger, and initializes
\mathcal{B}_{1}
with
\rho_{\textsc{Aux}}
.

•

\mathcal{B}_{1}
runs
\mathcal{A}_{1}
with auxuliary input
\rho_{\textsc{Aux}}
. When
\mathcal{A}_{1}
outputs a vector
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}
, the procedure
\mathcal{B}_{1}
checks if
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}=\mathbf{v}
. If true,
\mathcal{B}_{1}
outputs
1
. Else,
\mathcal{B}_{1}
outputs
0
otherwise.

Note that if
b=0
, the state
\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}}
prepared by
\mathcal{B}_{0}
corresponds to

\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}\\
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{y}\end{subarray}}\rho_{\sigma}(\mathbf{x})\,\omega_{q}^{\langle\mathbf{x},\lfloor\frac{q}{\nu}\rfloor\cdot\mathbf{v}\rangle}\,\ket{\mathbf{x}},

and thus matches the correct input from
\mathsf{H}_{0}
.
Moreover, if
b=1
, the state is collapsed and thus matches the correct input from
\mathsf{H}_{1}
. Crucially, the Pauli-Z operator
\mathbf{Z}_{q}^{\lfloor\frac{q}{\nu}\rfloor\cdot\mathbf{v}}
has no effect on the collapsed state and merely introduces a global phase.
Therefore,

\mathcal{B}=(\mathcal{B}_{0},\mathcal{B}_{1})
distinguishes
\mathsf{EvTargetCollapseExpt}_{\mathcal{B},\lambda}(1^{\lambda},b)
for
b\in{\{0,1\}}
with advantage at least
1/\mathrm{poly}(\lambda)
, which results in the desired contradiction.
∎

Because the vector
\mathbf{v}
is completely hidden from the view of the adversary in
\mathsf{H}_{1}
, the probability that
\mathcal{A}_{1}
guesses it correctly is at most
2^{-m}
, which is negligible. This proves the claim.
∎

Simultaneous search-to-decision reduction with classical revocation

We give a strengthening of our result in
Theorem

6.11
and state a
simultaneous
search-to-decision reduction with quantum auxiliary input which holds even if additionally require that a
classical revocation
procedure succeeds. This is essentially a classical revocation analogue of
Theorem

6.16
.

To prove the theorem, we require the following analogue of
1
.

Conjecture 2
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
. Then, there exist parameters (each parameterized by
\lambda
) such that
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
q
is a prime with
q=2^{o(n)}
,
m\geq 2n\log q
,
\sqrt{8m}<\sigma<q/\sqrt{8m}
,
\alpha\in(0,1)
with
1/\alpha=2^{o(n)}\cdot\sigma
and
\nu=64m^{2}
for which the following holds: for any
\mathsf{QPT}

\mathcal{A}
and
\mathsf{QPT}

\mathcal{C}
(and for a fixed algorithm
\mathcal{B}
), and for any
\mathrm{poly}(\lambda)
-sized quantum auxiliary input
\tau_{\lambda}
(which depends on
\lambda
):

\vline\,\Pr[1\leftarrow\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}}(1^{\lambda},0)]-\Pr[1\leftarrow\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}}(1^{\lambda},1)]\,\vline\leq\mathsf{negl}(\lambda),

where
\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}}(1^{\lambda},b)
is the cloning experiment in
Figure

8
.

\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}}(1^{\lambda},b)
:

1.

The challenger samples
(\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m},\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenTrap}(1^{n},1^{m},q)
and a Gaussian vector
\mathbf{x}_{0}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m},\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{2}}}
and lets
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{A}\cdot\mathbf{x}_{0}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q)
. Then, the challenger runs
\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}\leftarrow\mathsf{QSampGauss}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{y},\sigma)
, samples
\mathbf{v}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}{\{0,1\}}^{m}
and sends
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}})
to
\mathcal{A}
, where
\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}}=\mathbf{Z}_{q}^{\lfloor\frac{q}{\nu}\rfloor\cdot\mathbf{v}}\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}
.

2.

\mathcal{A}
receives
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}})
together with auxiliary input
\tau_{\lambda}
and generates a state
\rho_{\textsc{B}\textsc{C}}
in systems
BC
where
B
is classical, and sends
B
to
\mathcal{B}
and
C
to
\mathcal{C}
.

3.

The challenger sends
(\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{v},\sigma)
to
\mathcal{B}
and, depending on the value of
b
, the challenger sends the following to
\mathcal{C}
:

•

if
b=0
: the challenger samples
\mathbf{s}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
,
\mathbf{e}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z}^{m},\alpha q}
, lets
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{s}+\mathbf{e}
, and sends
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}^{\intercal}\mathbf{x}_{0}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q))
to
\mathcal{C}
.

•

if
b=0
: the challenger samples a uniformly random vector
\mathbf{u}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
and sends
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}^{\intercal}\mathbf{x}_{0}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q))
to
\mathcal{C}
.

4.

\mathcal{B}
receives as input
(\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{v},\sigma,\mathbf{w})
, where
\mathbf{w}
is the content of the classical register
B
, and applies
\mathsf{Revoke}(\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})
.

\mathcal{B}
outputs
\top
, if it succeeds, else outputs
\bot
.

5.

\mathcal{C}
receives as input
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}^{\intercal}\mathbf{x}_{0}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q),\textsc{C})
and outputs a bit
b^{\prime}
.

6.

The challenger outputs
1
, if
\mathcal{B}
outputs
\top
and
\mathcal{C}
outputs
b^{\prime}=b
. This is also the outcome of the experiment.

Figure 15:
The experiment for Conjecture 2.

We prove the following statement.

Theorem 7.9

(Simultaneous Search-to-Decision Reduction with Classical Revocation)
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
be the security parameter. Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
q
be a prime with
q=2^{o(n)}
,
m\geq 6n\log q
,
\sqrt{8m}<\sigma<q/\sqrt{8m}
,
\alpha,\beta\in(0,1)
with
\beta/\alpha=2^{o(n)}
,
1/\alpha=2^{o(n)}\cdot\sigma
and
\nu=64m^{2}
, such that the following holds: Let

\mathcal{A}=\{(\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}},\tau_{\lambda})\}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{N}}

be any non-uniform quantum algorithm consisting of a family of polynomial-sized quantum circuits

\Bigg\{\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{q}^{m}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B_{\lambda}})\allowbreak\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{R_{\lambda}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\textsc{aux}_{\lambda}})\Bigg\}_{\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m},\,\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}}

and polynomial-sized advice states
\tau_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{B_{\lambda}})
which are independent of
\mathbf{A}
.
Then, assuming
2
is true, the following statement holds for every
\mathsf{QPT}
distinguisher
\mathcal{D}
. Suppose there exists a function
\varepsilon(\lambda)=1/\mathrm{poly}(\lambda)
such that

\displaystyle\vline\,{\sf Pr}\left[1\leftarrow\mathsf{SimultSearchToDecisionExpt}^{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D}}(1^{\lambda},0)\right]-

\displaystyle\,{\sf Pr}\left[1\leftarrow\mathsf{SimultSearchToDecisionExpt}^{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D}}(1^{\lambda},1)\right]\,\vline=\varepsilon(\lambda).

\mathsf{SimultSearchToDecisionExpt}^{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D}}\left(1^{\lambda},b\right)
:

•

If
b=0
: output
{\sf simult}.{\sf lwe}.{\sf Dist}^{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D}}\left(1^{\lambda}\right)
defined in
Figure

17
.

•

If
b=1
: output
{\sf simult}.{\sf unif}.{\sf Dist}^{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D}}\left(1^{\lambda}\right)
defined in
Figure

18
.

Figure 16
:
The experiment
\mathsf{SimultSearchToDecisionExpt}^{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D}}\left(1^{\lambda},b\right)
.

Then, there exists a quantum extractor
\mathcal{E}
that takes as input
\mathbf{A}
,
\mathbf{y}
and system
Aux
of the state
\rho_{\textsc{R},\mathbf{\textsc{Aux}}}
and outputs a short vector in the coset
\Lambda_{q}^{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{A})
in time
\mathrm{poly}(\lambda,m,\sigma,q,1/\varepsilon)
such that

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{Revoke}(\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})=\top\\
\ \\
\bigwedge\\
\ \\
\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\rho_{\mathbf{\textsc{Aux}}})\,\in\,\Lambda_{q}^{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{A})\,\cap\,\mathcal{B}^{m}(\mathbf{0},\sigma\sqrt{\frac{m}{2}})\end{subarray}\,:\,\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenTrap}(1^{n},1^{m},q)\\
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)\\
\mathbf{v}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}{\{0,1\}}^{m},\,\,\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}}\leftarrow\mathbf{Z}_{q}^{\lfloor\frac{q}{\nu}\rfloor\cdot\mathbf{v}}\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}\\
|\mathbf{w}\rangle\langle\mathbf{w}|\otimes\rho_{\textsc{Aux}}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}|\otimes\tau_{\lambda})\end{subarray}\right]\geq 1/\mathrm{poly}(\varepsilon,1/q).

{\sf simult}.{\sf lwe}.{\sf Dist}^{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D}}\left(1^{\lambda}\right)
:

1.

Sample
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenTrap}(1^{n},1^{m},q)
.

2.

Generate
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)
.

3.

Sample
\mathbf{v}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}{\{0,1\}}^{m}
and compute
\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}}\leftarrow\mathbf{Z}_{q}^{\lfloor\frac{q}{\nu}\rfloor\cdot\mathbf{v}}\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}
.

4.

Generate
|\mathbf{w}\rangle\langle\mathbf{w}|\otimes\rho_{\textsc{Aux}}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}|\otimes\tau_{\lambda})
.

5.

Sample
\mathbf{s}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n},\mathbf{e}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z}^{m},\alpha q}
and
e^{\prime}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z},\beta q}
.

6.

Run
\mathsf{Revoke}(\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{w})
on
\mathbf{w}
. If it outputs
\top
, continue. Otherwise, output
\mathsf{Invalid}
.

7.

Run
b^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{s}^{\intercal}\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{e}^{\intercal},\mathbf{s}^{\intercal}\mathbf{y}+e^{\prime},\rho_{\textsc{Aux}})
. Output
b^{\prime}
.

Figure 17:
The distribution
{\sf simult}.{\sf lwe}.{\sf Dist}^{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D}}\left(1^{\lambda}\right)
.

{\sf simult}.{\sf unif}.{\sf Dist}^{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D}}\left(1^{\lambda}\right)
:

1.

Sample
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenTrap}(1^{n},1^{m},q)
.

2.

Generate
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)
.

3.

Sample
\mathbf{v}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}{\{0,1\}}^{m}
and compute
\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}}\leftarrow\mathbf{Z}_{q}^{\lfloor\frac{q}{\nu}\rfloor\cdot\mathbf{v}}\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}
.

4.

Generate
|\mathbf{w}\rangle\langle\mathbf{w}|\otimes\rho_{\textsc{Aux}}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}|\otimes\tau_{\lambda})
.

5.

Sample
\mathbf{s}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n},\mathbf{e}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z}^{m},\alpha q}
and
e^{\prime}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z},\beta q}
.

6.

Sample
\mathbf{u}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
and
r\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}
.

7.

Run
\mathsf{Revoke}(\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{w})
on
\mathbf{w}
. If it outputs
\top
, continue. Otherwise, output
\mathsf{Invalid}
.

8.

Run
b^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{u},r,\rho_{\textsc{Aux}})
. Output
b^{\prime}
.

Figure 18:
The distribution
{\sf simult}.{\sf unif}.{\sf Dist}^{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D}}\left(1^{\lambda}\right)
.

Proof.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
be the security parameter and let
\mathcal{A}=\{(\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}},\tau_{\lambda})\}_{\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}}

be a non-uniform quantum algorithm. Suppose that
\mathcal{D}
is a
\mathsf{QPT}
distinguisher with advantage
\varepsilon=1/\mathrm{poly}(\lambda)
.

To prove the claim, we consider the following sequence of hybrid distributions.

\mathsf{H}_{0}
: This is the distribution
{\sf simult}.{\sf lwe}.{\sf Dist}^{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D}}\left(1^{\lambda}\right)
in
Figure

17
.

\mathsf{H}_{1}
: This is the following distribution:

1.

Sample
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenTrap}(1^{n},1^{m},q)
.

2.

Sample a Gaussian vector
\mathbf{x}_{0}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m},\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{2}}}
and let
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{A}\cdot\mathbf{x}_{0}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q)
.

3.

Run
\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}\leftarrow\mathsf{QSampGauss}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{y},\sigma)
.

4.

Sample
\mathbf{v}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}{\{0,1\}}^{m}
and compute
\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}}\leftarrow\mathbf{Z}_{q}^{\lfloor\frac{q}{\nu}\rfloor\cdot\mathbf{v}}\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}
.

5.

Run
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}|\otimes\tau_{\lambda})
to generate a state
|\mathbf{w}\rangle\langle\mathbf{w}|\otimes\rho_{\mathbf{\textsc{aux}}}
.

6.

Run
\mathsf{Revoke}(\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})
. If it outputs
\top
, continue. Otherwise, output
\mathsf{Invalid}
.

7.

Sample
\mathbf{s}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
,
\mathbf{e}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z}^{m},\alpha q}
and
e^{\prime}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z},\beta q}
.
Let
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{s}+\mathbf{e}
.

8.

Run the distinguisher

\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}^{\intercal}\mathbf{x}_{0}+e^{\prime},\cdot)
on system
Aux
. Output
b^{\prime}
.

\mathsf{H}_{2}:
This is the following distribution:

1.

Sample
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenTrap}(1^{n},1^{m},q)
.

2.

Sample a Gaussian vector
\mathbf{x}_{0}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m},\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{2}}}
and let
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{A}\cdot\mathbf{x}_{0}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q)
.

3.

Run
\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}\leftarrow\mathsf{QSampGauss}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{y},\sigma)
.

4.

Sample
\mathbf{v}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}{\{0,1\}}^{m}
and compute
\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}}\leftarrow\mathbf{Z}_{q}^{\lfloor\frac{q}{\nu}\rfloor\cdot\mathbf{v}}\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}
.

5.

Run
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}|\otimes\tau_{\lambda})
to generate a state
|\mathbf{w}\rangle\langle\mathbf{w}|\otimes\rho_{\mathbf{\textsc{aux}}}
.

6.

Run
\mathsf{Revoke}(\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})
. If it outputs
\top
, continue. Otherwise, output
\mathsf{Invalid}
.

7.

Sample

\mathbf{u}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
and
e^{\prime}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z},\beta q}
.

8.

Run the distinguisher

\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}^{\intercal}\mathbf{x}_{0}+e^{\prime},\cdot)
on system
Aux
. Output
b^{\prime}
.

\mathsf{H}_{3}
: This is the distribution
{\sf simult}.{\sf unif}.{\sf Dist}^{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D}}\left(1^{\lambda}\right)
defined in
Figure

18
.

We now show the following:

Claim 7.10
.

Hybrids
\mathsf{H}_{0}
and
\mathsf{H}_{1}
are statistically indistinguishable. In other words,

\mathsf{H}_{0}\,\approx_{s}\,\mathsf{H}_{1}.

Proof.

Here, we invoke the
noise flooding
property in
Lemma

2.9
to argue that
\mathbf{e}^{\intercal}\mathbf{x}_{0}\ll e^{\prime}
holds with overwhelming probability for our choice of parameters.
Therefore, the distributions in
\mathsf{H}_{0}
and
\mathsf{H}_{1}
are computationally indistinguishable.
∎

Claim 7.11
.

Assuming
2
holds for our choice of parameters, the hybrids
\mathsf{H}_{1}
and
\mathsf{H}_{2}
are computationally indistinguishable,

\mathsf{H}_{1}\,\approx_{c}\,\mathsf{H}_{2}.

Proof.

This follows from directly from
2
, which essentially allows us to invoke a variant of the
\mathsf{LWE}
assumption, even when the procedure
\mathsf{Revoke}(\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})
is simultaneously taken into account. Here, we rely on the fact that during the reduction, we can simply sample
e^{\prime}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z},\beta q}
to produce an identically distributed challenge distribution.
∎

Recall that
\mathsf{H}_{0}
and
\mathsf{H}_{3}
can be distinguished with probability
\varepsilon=1/\mathrm{poly}(\lambda)
. We proved that the hybrids
\mathsf{H}_{0}
and
\mathsf{H}_{2}
are computationally indistinguishable. As a consequence, it holds that hybrids
\mathsf{H}_{2}
and
\mathsf{H}_{3}
can be distinguished with probability at least
\varepsilon-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
.

We leverage this to obtain a Goldreich-Levin reduction. Consider the following distinguisher.

\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\big(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{u},v,\rho\big)
:

Input:
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
,
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
,
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
,
v\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}
and
\rho\in L(\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{\textsc{Aux}}})
.

Output: A bit
b^{\prime}\in{\{0,1\}}
.

Procedure:

1.

Sample
e^{\prime}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z},\beta q}
.

2.

Output
b^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathcal{D}\big(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{u},v+e^{\prime},\rho\big)
.

Figure 19:
The distinguisher
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}\big(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{u},v,\rho\big)
.

Note that
r+e^{\prime}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q)
is uniform whenever
r\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}
and
e^{\prime}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z},\beta q}
. Therefore, our previous argument shows that there exists a negligible function
\eta
such that:

\displaystyle\vline\Pr\left[\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{Revoke}(\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})=\top\\
\ \\
\bigwedge\\
\ \\
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}^{\intercal}\mathbf{x}_{0},\rho_{\textsc{Aux}})=1\end{subarray}\,:\,\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenTrap}(1^{n},1^{m},q)\\
\mathbf{u}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}\\
\mathbf{x}_{0}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m},\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{2}}},\,\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{0}\text{ (mod $q$)}\\
\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}\leftarrow\mathsf{QSampGauss}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}},\sigma)\\
|\mathbf{w}\rangle\langle\mathbf{w}|\otimes\rho_{\mathbf{\textsc{Aux}}}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}|\otimes\tau_{\lambda})\end{subarray}\right]-

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{Revoke}(\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})=\top\\
\ \\
\bigwedge\\
\ \\
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{u},r,\rho_{\textsc{Aux}})=1\end{subarray}\,:\,\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenTrap}(1^{n},1^{m},q)\\
\mathbf{u}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m},r\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}\\
