\mathbf{x}_{0}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m},\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{2}}},\,\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{0}\text{ (mod $q$)}\\
\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}\leftarrow\mathsf{QSampGauss}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}},\sigma)\\
|\mathbf{w}\rangle\langle\mathbf{w}|\otimes\rho_{\mathbf{\textsc{Aux}}}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}|\otimes\tau_{\lambda})\end{subarray}\right]\vline\geq\varepsilon-\eta(\lambda).

From
Theorem

4.3
, it follows that there exists a Goldreich-Levin extractor
\mathcal{E}
running in time
T(\mathcal{E})=\mathrm{poly}(\lambda,n,m,\sigma,q,1/\varepsilon)
that outputs a short vector in
\Lambda_{q}^{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{A})
with probability at least

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{Revoke}(\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})=\top\\
\ \\
\bigwedge\\
\ \\
\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\rho_{\mathbf{\textsc{Aux}}})\,\in\,\Lambda_{q}^{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{A})\,\cap\,\mathcal{B}^{m}(\mathbf{0},\sigma\sqrt{\frac{m}{2}})\end{subarray}\,:\,\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenTrap}(1^{n},1^{m},q)\\
\mathbf{x}_{0}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m},\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{2}}},\,\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_{0}\text{ (mod $q$)}\\
\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}\leftarrow\mathsf{QSampGauss}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}},\sigma)\\
|\mathbf{w}\rangle\langle\mathbf{w}|\otimes\rho_{\mathbf{\textsc{Aux}}}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}|\otimes\tau_{\lambda})\end{subarray}\right]\geq 1/\mathrm{poly}(\varepsilon,1/q).

This proves the claim.
∎

Towards a proof of the conjecture.

We now give a proof of the
simultaneous
search-to-decision reduction (
Theorem

7.9
) from standard assumptions in the special case when revocation succeeds with overwhelming probability.

Theorem 7.12
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
q
be a prime with
q=2^{o(n)}
,
m\geq 6n\log q
,
\sqrt{8m}<\sigma<q/\sqrt{8m}
, and let
\alpha,\beta\in(0,1)
with
\beta/\alpha=2^{o(n)}
with
1/\alpha=2^{o(n)}\cdot\sigma
. Let
\nu=64m^{2}
. Let

\mathcal{A}=\{(\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}},\tau_{\lambda})\}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{N}}

be any non-uniform quantum algorithm consisting of a family of polynomial-sized quantum circuits

\Bigg\{\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{q}^{m}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B_{\lambda}})\allowbreak\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{R_{\lambda}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\textsc{aux}_{\lambda}})\Bigg\}_{\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m},\,\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}}

and polynomial-sized advice states
\tau_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{B_{\lambda}})
which are independent of
\mathbf{A}
such that

\Pr\left[\mathsf{Revoke}(\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})=\top\,:\,\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenTrap}(1^{n},1^{m},q)\\
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)\\
\mathbf{v}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}{\{0,1\}}^{m},\,\,\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}}\leftarrow\mathbf{Z}_{q}^{\lfloor\frac{q}{\nu}\rfloor\cdot\mathbf{v}}\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}\\
|\mathbf{w}\rangle\langle\mathbf{w}|\otimes\rho_{\textsc{Aux}}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}|\otimes\tau_{\lambda})\end{subarray}\right]=1-\mu(\lambda),

for some negligle function
\mu(\lambda)
.
Then,
assuming the quantum hardness of the
\mathsf{LWE}_{n,q,\alpha q}^{m}
assumption, the following holds for every
\mathsf{QPT}
distinguisher
\mathcal{D}
. Suppose that there exists a function
\varepsilon(\lambda)=1/\mathrm{poly}(\lambda)
such that

\displaystyle\vline\,{\sf Pr}\left[1\leftarrow\mathsf{SimultSearchToDecisionExpt}^{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D}}(1^{\lambda},0)\right]-

\displaystyle\,{\sf Pr}\left[1\leftarrow\mathsf{SimultSearchToDecisionExpt}^{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D}}(1^{\lambda},1)\right]\,\vline=\varepsilon(\lambda).

Then, there exists a quantum extractor
\mathcal{E}
that takes as input
\mathbf{A}
,
\mathbf{y}
and system
Aux
of the state
\rho_{\textsc{R},\mathbf{\textsc{Aux}}}
and outputs a short vector in the coset
\Lambda_{q}^{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{A})
in time
\mathrm{poly}(\lambda,m,\sigma,q,1/\varepsilon)
such that

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{Revoke}(\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})\vskip 0.56905pt\\
\bigwedge\vskip 0.56905pt\\
\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\textsc{Aux})\,\,\in\,\,\Lambda_{q}^{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{A})\,\cap\,\mathcal{B}^{m}(\mathbf{0},\sigma\sqrt{\frac{m}{2}})\end{subarray}\,:\,\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenTrap}(1^{n},1^{m},q)\\
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)\\
\mathbf{v}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}{\{0,1\}}^{m},\,\,\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}}\leftarrow\mathbf{Z}_{q}^{\lfloor\frac{q}{\nu}\rfloor\cdot\mathbf{v}}\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}\\
|\mathbf{w}\rangle\langle\mathbf{w}|\otimes\rho_{\textsc{Aux}}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}|\otimes\tau_{\lambda})\end{subarray}\right]\,\geq\,\mathrm{poly}(\varepsilon,1/q).

Proof.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
and suppose that there exists an adversary consisting of a pair of
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithms
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D})
and a function
\varepsilon(\lambda)=1/\mathrm{poly}(\lambda)
such that

\displaystyle\vline\Pr\left[\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{Revoke}(\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})=\top\\
\ \\
\bigwedge\\
\ \\
\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{s}^{\intercal}\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{e}^{\intercal},\mathbf{s}^{\intercal}\mathbf{y}+e^{\prime},\rho_{\mathbf{\textsc{Aux}}})=1\end{subarray}\,:\,\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenTrap}(1^{n},1^{m},q)\\
\mathbf{v}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}{\{0,1\}}^{m},\,\mathbf{s}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}\\
\mathbf{e}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z}^{m},\,\alpha q},\,e^{\prime}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z},\,\beta q}\\
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)\\
\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}}\leftarrow\mathbf{Z}_{q}^{\lfloor\frac{q}{\nu}\rfloor\cdot\mathbf{v}}\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}\\
|\mathbf{w}\rangle\langle\mathbf{w}|\otimes\rho_{\textsc{Aux}}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}|\otimes\tau_{\lambda})\end{subarray}\right]-

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{Revoke}(\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})=\top\\
\ \\
\bigwedge\\
\ \\
\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{u},r,\rho_{\mathbf{\textsc{Aux}}})=1\end{subarray}\,:\,\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenTrap}(1^{n},1^{m},q)\\
\mathbf{v}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}{\{0,1\}}^{m},\mathbf{u}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m},\,r\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}\\
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)\\
\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}}\leftarrow\mathbf{Z}_{q}^{\lfloor\frac{q}{\nu}\rfloor\cdot\mathbf{v}}\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}\\
|\mathbf{w}\rangle\langle\mathbf{w}|\otimes\rho_{\textsc{Aux}}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}|\otimes\tau_{\lambda})\end{subarray}\right]\vline=\varepsilon(\lambda).

From
Lemma

6.10
, it follows that there exists a
\mathsf{QPT}
distinguisher
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}
that succeeds on the reduced state on
Aux
alone (where we drop the condition that
\mathsf{Revoke}(\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})
outputs
\top
). In other words, there exists
\bar{\epsilon}=1/\mathrm{poly}(\lambda)
such that

\displaystyle\vline\Pr\left[\tilde{\mathcal{D}}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{s}^{\intercal}\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{e}^{\intercal},\mathbf{s}^{\intercal}\mathbf{y}+e^{\prime},\rho_{\mathbf{\textsc{Aux}}})=1\,:\,\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenTrap}(1^{n},1^{m},q)\\
\mathbf{v}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}{\{0,1\}}^{m},\,\mathbf{s}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}\\
\mathbf{e}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z}^{m},\,\alpha q},\,e^{\prime}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z},\,\beta q}\\
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)\\
\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}}\leftarrow\mathbf{Z}_{q}^{\lfloor\frac{q}{\nu}\rfloor\cdot\mathbf{v}}\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}\\
|\mathbf{w}\rangle\langle\mathbf{w}|\otimes\rho_{\textsc{Aux}}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}|\otimes\tau_{\lambda})\end{subarray}\right]-

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\tilde{\mathcal{D}}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{u},r,\rho_{\mathbf{\textsc{Aux}}})=1\,:\,\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenTrap}(1^{n},1^{m},q)\\
\mathbf{v}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}{\{0,1\}}^{m},\mathbf{u}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m},\,r\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}\\
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)\\
\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}}\leftarrow\mathbf{Z}_{q}^{\lfloor\frac{q}{\nu}\rfloor\cdot\mathbf{v}}\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}\\
|\mathbf{w}\rangle\langle\mathbf{w}|\otimes\rho_{\textsc{Aux}}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}|\otimes\tau_{\lambda})\end{subarray}\right]\vline=\bar{\varepsilon}(\lambda).

Because
\tilde{\mathcal{D}}
succeeds with distinguishing advantage
\bar{\varepsilon}=1/\mathrm{poly}(\lambda)
, we can use
Theorem

6.11
to argue that
there exists a quantum extractor
\mathcal{E}
that takes as input a set of good inputs
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\rho_{\textsc{Aux}})
, and outputs a short vector in the coset
\Lambda_{q}^{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{A})
in time
\mathrm{poly}(\lambda,m,\sigma,q,1/\bar{\varepsilon})
such that

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\rho_{\mathbf{\textsc{Aux}}})=\mathbf{x}_{0}\vskip 2.84526pt\\
\bigwedge\vskip 0.28453pt\\
{\mathbf{x}_{0}}\,\,\in\,\,\Lambda_{q}^{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{A})\,\cap\,\mathcal{B}^{m}(\mathbf{0},\sigma\sqrt{\frac{m}{2}})\end{subarray}\,:\,\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenTrap}(1^{n},1^{m},q)\\
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)\\
\mathbf{v}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}{\{0,1\}}^{m},\,\,\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}}\leftarrow\mathbf{Z}_{q}^{\lfloor\frac{q}{\nu}\rfloor\cdot\mathbf{v}}\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}\\
|\mathbf{w}\rangle\langle\mathbf{w}|\otimes\rho_{\textsc{Aux}}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}|\otimes\tau_{\lambda})\end{subarray}\right]\geq\mathrm{poly}(\bar{\varepsilon},1/q).

Recall that revocation succeeds with overwhelming probability, i.e.,

\Pr\left[\mathsf{Revoke}(\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})=\top\,:\,\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenTrap}(1^{n},1^{m},q)\\
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)\\
\mathbf{v}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}{\{0,1\}}^{m},\,\,\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}}\leftarrow\mathbf{Z}_{q}^{\lfloor\frac{q}{\nu}\rfloor\cdot\mathbf{v}}\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}\\
|\mathbf{w}\rangle\langle\mathbf{w}|\otimes\rho_{\textsc{Aux}}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}|\otimes\tau_{\lambda})\end{subarray}\right]=1-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

Therefore, we can use Bonferroni’s inequality to argue that

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{Revoke}(\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})=\top\vskip 0.56905pt\\
\bigwedge\vskip 0.56905pt\\
\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\textsc{Aux})\,\,\in\,\,\Lambda_{q}^{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{A})\,\cap\,\mathcal{B}^{m}(\mathbf{0},\sigma\sqrt{\frac{m}{2}})\end{subarray}\,:\,\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenTrap}(1^{n},1^{m},q)\\
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)\\
\mathbf{v}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}{\{0,1\}}^{m},\,\,\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}}\leftarrow\mathbf{Z}_{q}^{\lfloor\frac{q}{\nu}\rfloor\cdot\mathbf{v}}\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}\\
|\mathbf{w}\rangle\langle\mathbf{w}|\otimes\rho_{\textsc{Aux}}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\mathbf{A}\rangle\langle\mathbf{A}|\otimes|\mathbf{y}\rangle\langle\mathbf{y}|\otimes|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}|\otimes\tau_{\lambda})\end{subarray}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\Pr\left[\mathsf{Revoke}(\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})=\top\,:\,\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenTrap}(1^{n},1^{m},q)\\
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)\\
\mathbf{v}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}{\{0,1\}}^{m},\,\,\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}}\leftarrow\mathbf{Z}_{q}^{\lfloor\frac{q}{\nu}\rfloor\cdot\mathbf{v}}\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}\\
|\mathbf{w}\rangle\langle\mathbf{w}|\otimes\rho_{\textsc{Aux}}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}|\otimes\tau_{\lambda})\end{subarray}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\Pr\left[\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\rho_{\textsc{Aux}})\,\,\in\,\,\Lambda_{q}^{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{A})\,\cap\,\mathcal{B}^{m}(\mathbf{0},\sigma\sqrt{m/2})\,:\,\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenTrap}(1^{n},1^{m},q)\\
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)\\
\mathbf{v}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}{\{0,1\}}^{m},\,\,\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}}\leftarrow\mathbf{Z}_{q}^{\lfloor\frac{q}{\nu}\rfloor\cdot\mathbf{v}}\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}\\
|\mathbf{w}\rangle\langle\mathbf{w}|\otimes\rho_{\textsc{Aux}}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}|\otimes\tau_{\lambda})\end{subarray}\right]-1

\displaystyle\geq\Pr\left[\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\rho_{\textsc{Aux}})\,\,\in\,\,\Lambda_{q}^{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{A})\,\cap\,\mathcal{B}^{m}(\mathbf{0},\sigma\sqrt{m/2})\,:\,\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenTrap}(1^{n},1^{m},q)\\
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)\\
\mathbf{v}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}{\{0,1\}}^{m},\,\,\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}}\leftarrow\mathbf{Z}_{q}^{\lfloor\frac{q}{\nu}\rfloor\cdot\mathbf{v}}\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}\\
|\mathbf{w}\rangle\langle\mathbf{w}|\otimes\rho_{\textsc{Aux}}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}|\otimes\tau_{\lambda})\end{subarray}\right]-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)

\displaystyle\geq\mathrm{poly}(\varepsilon,1/q).

This proves the claim.
∎

Proof of security.

We can show that the Dual-Regev scheme with classical revocation achieves our notion key-revocable security from
Definition

7.2
, assuming either
Theorem

7.9
is true, or using
Theorem

7.12
based on
\mathsf{LWE}
/
\mathsf{SIS}

in case revocation succeeds with overwhelming probability.

To carry out the proof, we show that a successful key-revocation adversary allows us to break the uncertainty principle for Gaussian superpositions in
Theorem

7.7
. The idea is the following: Suppose there exists an algorithm
\mathcal{D}
that can distinguish Dual-Regev from uniform given
\rho_{\textsc{Aux}}
in the key-revocation experiment (conditioned on
\mathsf{Revoke}
succeeding on certificate
\mathbf{w}
). Then we can use it to extract (using Goldreich-Levin) a short pre-image
\mathbf{x}_{0}
from the internal state
\rho_{\textsc{Aux}}
which we can send to the challenger as part of the experiment in
Theorem

7.7
. Moreover, because the revocation certificate
\mathbf{w}
passes verification
17
17

17

Here, one can think of it as essentially being an LWE encryption
\mathbf{w}^{\intercal}=\hat{\mathbf{s}}^{\intercal}\mathbf{A}+\lfloor\frac{q}{\nu}\rfloor\cdot\mathbf{v}^{\intercal}+\hat{\mathbf{e}}^{\intercal}
of the string
\mathbf{v}
.
, it must yield
\mathbf{v}
when
\mathbf{w}
is decrypted using a short trapdoor basis (i.e., by running
\mathsf{Revoke}
).
Fortunately, once we present a pre-image
\mathbf{x}_{0}
in the second half of the experiment, we are allowed to be computationally unbounded; meaning, we can simply decode
\mathbf{w}
in exponential time (i.e. by breaking
\mathsf{LWE}
, or by finding a trapdoor that can be used to decode
\mathbf{w}
using
\mathsf{Revoke}
). Therefore, we obtain an adversary that ends up finding both a pre-image
\mathbf{x}_{0}
and
\mathbf{v}
at the same time, which violates
Theorem

7.7
.

Our first result concerns
(\mathsf{negl}(\lambda),\mathsf{negl}(\lambda))
-security, i.e., where we assume that revocation succeeds with overwhelming probability.

Theorem 7.13
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
q
be a prime with
q=2^{o(n)}
and
m=\lceil 6n\log q\rceil
, each parameterized by
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
q/\sqrt{8m}>\sigma>\sqrt{8m}
and let
\alpha,\beta\in(0,1)
be noise ratios chosen such that
\beta/\alpha=2^{o(n)}
and
1/\alpha=2^{o(n)}\cdot\sigma
. Let
\nu=64m^{2}
.
Then, assuming the subexponential hardness of the
\mathsf{LWE}_{n,q,\alpha q}^{m}

and
\mathsf{SIS}_{n,q,\sigma\sqrt{2m}}^{m}
problems, the scheme

\mathsf{CRevDual}=(\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec},\mathsf{Delete},\mathsf{Revoke})
in
2

is a
(\mathsf{negl}(\lambda),\mathsf{negl}(\lambda))
-secure key-revocable public-key encryption scheme with classical revocation.

Proof.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
and suppose that there exists an adversary consisting of a pair of
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithms
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D})
and a function
\varepsilon(\lambda)=1/\mathrm{poly}(\lambda)
such that

\displaystyle\vline\Pr\left[\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{Revoke}(\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})=\top\\
\ \\
\bigwedge\\
\ \\
\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{s}^{\intercal}\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{e}^{\intercal},\mathbf{s}^{\intercal}\mathbf{y}+e^{\prime},\rho_{\mathbf{\textsc{Aux}}})=1\end{subarray}\,:\,\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenTrap}(1^{n},1^{m},q)\\
\mathbf{v}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}{\{0,1\}}^{m},\,\mathbf{s}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}\\
\mathbf{e}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z}^{m},\,\alpha q},\,e^{\prime}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z},\,\beta q}\\
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)\\
\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}}\leftarrow\mathbf{Z}_{q}^{\lfloor\frac{q}{\nu}\rfloor\cdot\mathbf{v}}\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}\\
|\mathbf{w}\rangle\langle\mathbf{w}|\otimes\rho_{\textsc{Aux}}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}|\otimes\tau_{\lambda})\end{subarray}\right]-

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{Revoke}(\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})=\top\\
\ \\
\bigwedge\\
\ \\
\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{u},r,\rho_{\mathbf{\textsc{Aux}}})=1\end{subarray}\,:\,\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenTrap}(1^{n},1^{m},q)\\
\mathbf{v}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}{\{0,1\}}^{m},\mathbf{u}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m},\,r\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}\\
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)\\
\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}}\leftarrow\mathbf{Z}_{q}^{\lfloor\frac{q}{\nu}\rfloor\cdot\mathbf{v}}\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}\\
|\mathbf{w}\rangle\langle\mathbf{w}|\otimes\rho_{\textsc{Aux}}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}|\otimes\tau_{\lambda})\end{subarray}\right]\vline=\varepsilon(\lambda).

We now show how to use
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D})
to violate the uncertainty principle for Gaussian states from
Theorem

7.7
.
Consider the adversary
(\mathcal{B}_{0},\mathcal{B}_{1})
consisting of the following procedures:

•

\mathcal{B}_{0}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}})
: run the algorithm
\mathcal{A}
to generate

|\mathbf{w}\rangle\langle\mathbf{w}|\otimes\rho_{\textsc{Aux}}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}|\otimes\tau_{\lambda}).

Then, run the Goldreich-Levin extractor
\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\rho_{\mathbf{\textsc{aux}}})
from
Theorem

7.12
(instantiated using
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D}
) and let
\mathbf{x}_{0}
be the outcome. Send
\mathbf{x}_{0}
to the challenger and initialize
{\mathcal{B}_{1}}
with
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{w})
.

•

\mathcal{B}_{1}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{w})
: compute (in exponential time) a
(20n\log q)
-good trapdoor basis
\hat{\mathbf{T}}_{\mathbf{A}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m\times m}
for the matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
as in
Definition

7.3
with the properties that

\mathbf{A}\cdot\hat{\mathbf{T}}_{\mathbf{A}}=\mathbf{0}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q)\quad\text{ and }\quad\|\hat{\mathbf{T}}_{\mathbf{A}}\|\leq 20n\log q.

Then, use
\hat{\mathbf{T}}_{\mathbf{A}}
to do the following:

1.

Compute
\mathbf{c}^{\intercal}=\mathbf{w}^{\intercal}\cdot\hat{\mathbf{T}}_{\mathbf{A}}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q)
.

2.

Compute
\mathbf{d}^{\intercal}=\mathbf{c}^{\intercal}\cdot\hat{\mathbf{T}}_{\mathbf{A}}^{-1}
, where
\hat{\mathbf{T}}_{\mathbf{A}}^{-1}
is the inverse matrix of
\hat{\mathbf{T}}_{\mathbf{A}}
over
\mathbb{R}
.

3.

For
i\in[m]
, let
v^{\prime}_{i}=0
, if
d_{i}\in[-q/\sigma,q/\sigma]
, and let
v^{\prime}_{i}=1
, otherwise.

4.

Output
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}=(v^{\prime}_{1},\dots,v^{\prime}_{m})
.

Because
\mathcal{D}
succeeds with distinguishing advantage
\varepsilon=1/\mathrm{poly}(\lambda)
, it follows from
Theorem

7.12
the extractor outputs a short vector in the coset
\Lambda_{q}^{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{A})
in time
\mathrm{poly}(\lambda,m,\sigma,q,1/\varepsilon)
such that

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{Revoke}(\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w})\vskip 0.56905pt\\
\bigwedge\vskip 0.56905pt\\
\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\textsc{Aux})\,\,\in\,\,\Lambda_{q}^{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{A})\,\cap\,\mathcal{B}^{m}(\mathbf{0},\sigma\sqrt{\frac{m}{2}})\end{subarray}\,:\,\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{A}})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenTrap}(1^{n},1^{m},q)\\
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)\\
\mathbf{v}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}{\{0,1\}}^{m},\,\,\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}}\leftarrow\mathbf{Z}_{q}^{\lfloor\frac{q}{\nu}\rfloor\cdot\mathbf{v}}\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}\\
|\mathbf{w}\rangle\langle\mathbf{w}|\otimes\rho_{\textsc{Aux}}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\lambda,\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}}(|\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{v}}|\otimes\tau_{\lambda})\end{subarray}\right]\,\geq\,\mathrm{poly}(\varepsilon,1/q).

Finally, because
\hat{\mathbf{T}}_{\mathbf{A}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m\times m}
is a
(20n\log q)
-good trapdoor basis for
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
, this implies that the probability that
\mathcal{B}_{1}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{w})
correctly recovers
\mathbf{v}
is also at least
1/\mathrm{poly}(\lambda)
.
Therefore,

(\mathcal{B}_{0},\mathcal{B}_{1})
has non-negligible probability of finding both the correct certificate
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}=\mathbf{v}
as well as a short pre-image
\mathbf{x}_{0}
such that
\mathbf{A}\cdot\mathbf{x}_{0}=\mathbf{y}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q)
and
\|\mathbf{x}_{0}\|\leq\sigma\sqrt{m/2}
. This proves the claim.
∎

Finally, we obtain the following stronger notion of
(\mathsf{negl}(\lambda),1-1/\mathrm{poly}(\lambda))
-security, assuming
Theorem

7.9
is true.

Theorem 7.14
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
q
be a prime with
q=2^{o(n)}
and
m=\lceil 6n\log q\rceil
, each parameterized by
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
q/\sqrt{8m}>\sigma>\sqrt{8m}
and let
\alpha,\beta\in(0,1)
be noise ratios chosen such that
\beta/\alpha=2^{o(n)}
and
1/\alpha=2^{o(n)}\cdot\sigma
. Let
\nu=64m^{2}
.
Then, assuming
Theorem

7.9
, the scheme

\mathsf{CRevDual}=(\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec},\mathsf{Delete},\mathsf{Revoke})
in
2

is a
(\mathsf{negl}(\lambda),1-1/\mathrm{poly}(\lambda))
-secure key-revocable public-key encryption scheme with classical revocation.

Proof.

The proof is analogous to
Theorem

7.13
, except that we invoke
Theorem

7.9
instead of
Theorem

7.12
.
∎

8
Key-Revocable Fully Homomorphic Encryption

In this section, we describe our key-revocable (leveled) fully homomorphic encryption scheme from
\mathsf{LWE}
which is based on the so-called
\mathsf{DualGSW}
scheme used by Mahadev
[
Mah18
]
which itself is a variant of the homomorphic encryption scheme by Gentry, Sahai and Waters
[
GSW13
]
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
be the security parameter. Suppose we would like to evaluate
L
-depth circuits consisting of
\mathsf{NAND}
gates. We choose
n(\lambda,L)\gg L
and a prime
q=2^{o(n)}
. Then, for integer parameters
m\geq 2n\log q
and
N=(m+1)\cdot\lceil\log q\rceil
, we let
\mathbf{I}
be the
(m+1)\times(m+1)
identity matrix and let

\mathbf{G}=[\mathbf{I}\,\|\,2\mathbf{I}\,\|\,\dots\,\|\,2^{\lceil\log q\rceil-1}\mathbf{I}]\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(m+1)\times N}
denote the so-called
gadget matrix
which converts a binary representation of a vector back to its original vector representation over the field
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
.
Note that the associated (non-linear) inverse operation
\mathbf{G}^{-1}
converts vectors in
\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m+1}
to their binary representation in
{\{0,1\}}^{N}
. In other words, we have that
\mathbf{G}\circ\mathbf{G}^{-1}
acts as the identity operator.

8.1
Construction

We now construct a key-revocable Dual-Regev (leveled) fully homomorphic encryption scheme based on our key-revocable Dual-Regev public-key scheme.

Remark 8.1
.

While the construction in this section can be readily adapted to feature
classical revocation
via
2
, we choose to focus on
quantum revocation
for simplicity by making use of our Dual-Regev scheme from
1
.

We are now ready to state our construction.

Construction 3

(Key-Revocable
DualGSW
encryption)
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
be the security parameter.
The scheme
\mathsf{RevDualGSW}=(\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec},\mathsf{Eval},\mathsf{Revoke})
consists of the following
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithms:

\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda},1^{L})\rightarrow(\mathsf{PK},\rho_{\mathsf{SK}}):

sample a pair
(\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m},\mathsf{td}_{\mathbf{A}})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenTrap}(1^{n},1^{m},q)
and generate a Gaussian superposition
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)
with

\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}\,\,=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}\\
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{y}\end{subarray}}\rho_{\sigma}(\mathbf{x})\,\ket{\mathbf{x}},

for some
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
.
Output
\mathsf{PK}=(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y})
,
\rho_{\mathsf{SK}}=\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}
and
\mathsf{MSK}=\mathsf{td}_{\mathbf{A}}
.

\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{PK},\mu):
to encrypt
\mu\in{\{0,1\}}
, parse
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y})\leftarrow\mathsf{PK}
, sample a random matrix
\mathbf{S}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times N}
and
\mathbf{E}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z}^{m\times N},\,\alpha q}
and row vector
\mathbf{e}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z}^{N},\,\beta q}
, and
output the ciphertext

\mathsf{CT}=\left[\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{S}+\mathbf{E}\vskip 2.84526pt\\
\hline\cr\vskip 2.84526pt\\
\mathbf{y}^{\intercal}\mathbf{S}+\mathbf{e}\end{subarray}\right]+\mu\cdot\mathbf{G}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q)\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(m+1)\times N}.

\mathsf{Eval}(\mathsf{CT}_{0},\mathsf{CT}_{1}):
to apply a
\mathsf{NAND}
gate on a ciphertext pair
\mathsf{CT}_{0}
and
\mathsf{CT}_{1}
, output the matrix

\mathbf{G}-\mathsf{CT}_{0}\cdot\mathbf{G}^{-1}(\mathsf{CT}_{1})\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q)\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(m+1)\times N}.

\mathsf{Dec}(\rho_{\mathsf{SK}},\mathsf{CT})\rightarrow{\{0,1\}}:
to decrypt
\mathsf{CT}
,
apply the unitary
U:\ket{\mathbf{x}}\ket{0}\rightarrow\ket{\mathbf{x}}\ket{(-\mathbf{x},1)\cdot\mathsf{CT}_{N}}
on input
\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}\leftarrow\rho_{\mathsf{SK}}
, where
\mathsf{CT}_{N}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m+1}
is the
N
-th column of
\mathsf{CT}
, and measure the second register in the computational basis. Output
0
, if the measurement outcome
is closer to
0
than to
\lfloor\frac{q}{2}\rfloor
,
and output
1
, otherwise.

\mathsf{Revoke}(\mathsf{MSK},\mathsf{PK},\rho)\rightarrow\{\top,\bot\}
: on input
\mathsf{td}_{\mathbf{A}}\leftarrow\mathsf{MSK}
and
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y})\leftarrow\mathsf{PK}
, apply the projective measurement
\{\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}\!\bra{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},I-\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}\!\bra{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}\}
onto
\rho
using
\mathsf{SampGauss}(\mathbf{A},\mathsf{td}_{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{y},\sigma)
in Algorithm
2
. Output
\top
if the measurement is successful, and output
\bot
otherwise.

8.2
Proof of security

\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda},b)
:

1.

The challenger samples
(\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m},\mathsf{td}_{\mathbf{A}})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenTrap}(1^{n},1^{m},q)
and generates

\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}\,\,=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}\\
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{y}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q)\end{subarray}}\rho_{\sigma}(\mathbf{x})\,\ket{\mathbf{x}},

for some
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
, by running
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)
. The challenger lets
\mathsf{MSK}\leftarrow\mathsf{td}_{\mathbf{A}}
and
\mathsf{PK}\leftarrow(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y})
and sends
\rho_{\mathsf{SK}}\leftarrow\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}
to the adversary
\mathcal{A}
.

2.

\mathcal{A}
generates a (possibly entangled) bipartite state
\rho_{R,\mathbf{\textsc{aux}}}
in systems
\mathcal{H}_{R}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\textsc{Aux}}
with
\mathcal{H}_{R}=\mathcal{H}_{q}^{m}
, returns system
R
and holds onto the auxiliary system
Aux
.

3.

The challenger runs
\mathsf{Revoke}(\mathsf{MSK},\mathsf{PK},\rho_{R})
, where
\rho_{R}
is the reduced state in system
R
. If the outcome is
\top
, the game continues. Otherwise, output
Invalid
.

4.

\mathcal{A}
submits a plaintext bit
\mu\in{\{0,1\}}
.

5.

The challenger does the following depending on
b\in{\{0,1\}}
:

•

if
b=0
: The challenger samples a random matrix
\mathbf{S}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times N}
and errors
\mathbf{E}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z}^{m\times N},\,\alpha q}
and row vector
\mathbf{e}\sim D_{\mathbb{Z}^{N},\,\beta q}
, and
outputs the ciphertext

\mathsf{CT}=\left[\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{S}+\mathbf{E}\vskip 2.84526pt\\
\hline\cr\vskip 2.84526pt\\
\mathbf{y}^{\intercal}\mathbf{S}+\mathbf{e}\end{subarray}\right]+\mu\cdot\mathbf{G}\,\,\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(m+1)\times N}.

•

if
b=1
: the challenger samples a matrix
\mathbf{U}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m\times N}
and row vector
r\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{N}
uniformly at random, and sends the following to
\mathcal{A}
:

\left[\begin{subarray}{c}\,\mathbf{U}\,\vskip 2.84526pt\\
\hline\cr\vskip 2.84526pt\\
\mathbf{r}\end{subarray}\right]\,\,\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(m+1)\times N}.

6.

\mathcal{A}
returns a bit
b^{\prime}\in{\{0,1\}}
.

Figure 20:
The key-revocable security experiment according to
Definition

5.3
.

Our first result on the security of
3
concerns
(\mathsf{negl}(\lambda),\mathsf{negl}(\lambda))
-security, i.e., we assume that revocation succeeds with overwhelming probability.

Theorem 8.2
.

Let
L
be an upper bound on the
\mathsf{NAND}
-depth of the circuit which is to be evaluated. Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
q
be a prime modulus with
n=n(\lambda,L)\gg L
,
q=2^{o(n)}
and
m\geq 2n\log q
, each parameterized by the security parameter
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
N=(m+1)\cdot\lceil\log q\rceil
be an integer. Let
q/\sqrt{8m}>\sigma>\sqrt{8m}
and let
\alpha,\beta\in(0,1)
be parameters such that
\beta/\alpha=2^{o(n)}
and
1/\alpha=2^{o(n)}\cdot\sigma
.
Then, assuming the subexponential hardness of the
\mathsf{LWE}_{n,q,\alpha q}^{m}

and
\mathsf{SIS}_{n,q,\sigma\sqrt{2m}}^{m}
problems, the scheme

\mathsf{RevDualGSW}=(\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec},\mathsf{Eval},\mathsf{Revoke})
in
3

is a
(\mathsf{negl}(\lambda),\mathsf{negl}(\lambda))
-secure key-revocable (leveled) fully homomorphic encryption scheme according to
Definition

5.3
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
\mathsf{QPT}
adversary and suppose that

\vline\,\Pr\left[1\leftarrow\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda},0)\right]-\Pr\left[1\leftarrow\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda},1)\right]\,\vline=\epsilon(\lambda),

for some
\varepsilon(\lambda)
with respect to
\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda},b)
in
Figure

20
. Note that the
RevDualGSW
ciphertext can (up to an additive shift) be thought of as a column-wise concatenation of
N
-many independent ciphertexts of our key-revocable Dual-Regev scheme in
1
.
Therefore, we can invoke
Theorem

6.1
in order to argue that
\varepsilon(\lambda)
is at most negligible.

∎

Our second result concerns
(\mathsf{negl}(\lambda),1-1/\mathrm{poly}(\lambda))
-security, i.e., we do not make any requirements on the success probability of revocation. Here, we need to invoke
Theorem

6.16
.

Theorem 8.3
.

Let
L
be an upper bound on the
\mathsf{NAND}
-depth of the circuit which is to be evaluated. Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
q
be a prime modulus with
n=n(\lambda,L)\gg L
,
q=2^{o(n)}
and
m\geq 2n\log q
, each parameterized by the security parameter
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
N=(m+1)\cdot\lceil\log q\rceil
be an integer. Let
q/\sqrt{8m}>\sigma>\sqrt{8m}
and let
\alpha,\beta\in(0,1)
be parameters such that
\beta/\alpha=2^{o(n)}
and
1/\alpha=2^{o(n)}\cdot\sigma
.
Then, assuming assuming
Theorem

6.16
, the scheme

\mathsf{RevDualGSW}=(\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec},\mathsf{Eval},\mathsf{Revoke})
in
3

is a
(\mathsf{negl}(\lambda),1-1/\mathrm{poly}(\lambda))
-secure key-revocable (leveled) fully homomorphic encryption scheme according to
Definition

5.3
.

Proof.

The proof is the same as in the theorem before, except that we invoke
Theorem

6.2
instead of
Theorem

6.1
in order to argue security.
∎

9
Revocable Pseudorandom Functions

In this section, we introduce the notion of
key-revocable
pseudorandom functions (or simply, called
revocable
) and present the first construction from (quantum hardness of) learning with errors.

9.1
Definition

Let us first recall the traditional notion of
\mathsf{PRF}
security
[
GGM86
]
, defined as follows.

Definition 9.1

(Pseudorandom Function)
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
and
\kappa(\lambda),\ell(\lambda)
and
\ell^{\prime}(\lambda)
be polynomials. A (post-quantum) pseudorandom function
(\mathsf{pqPRF})
is a pair
({\sf Gen},\mathsf{PRF})
of
\mathsf{PPT}
algorithms given by

•

{\sf Gen}(1^{\lambda}):
On input
1^{\lambda}
, it outputs a key
k\in{\{0,1\}}^{\kappa}
.

•

\mathsf{PRF}(k,x):
On input
k\in{\{0,1\}}^{\kappa}
and
x\in{\{0,1\}}^{\ell}
, it outputs a value
y\in{\{0,1\}}^{\ell^{\prime}}
.

with the property that, for any
\mathsf{QPT}
distinguisher
\mathcal{D}
, we have

\displaystyle\vline\,\Pr\left[\mathcal{D}^{\mathsf{PRF}(k,\cdot)}(1^{\lambda})=1]\,:\,k\leftarrow{\sf Gen}(1^{\lambda})\right]-\Pr\left[\mathcal{D}^{F(\cdot)}(1^{\lambda})=1]\,:\,F\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathcal{F}^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}\right]\,\vline\,\leq\,\mathsf{negl}(\lambda),

where
\mathcal{F}^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}
is the set of all functions with domain
{\{0,1\}}^{\ell}
and range
{\{0,1\}}^{\ell^{\prime}}
.

We now present a formal definition of revocable pseudorandom functions below.

Definition 9.2

(Revocable Pseudorandom Function)
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
be the security parameter and let
\kappa(\lambda),\ell(\lambda)
and
\ell^{\prime}(\lambda)
be polynomials.
A revocable pseudorandom function
(\mathsf{rPRF})
is a scheme

({\sf Gen},{\sf PRF},{\sf Eval},\mathsf{Revoke})
consisting of the following efficient algorithms:

•

{\sf Gen}(1^{\lambda})
: on input the security parameter
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
, it outputs a
{\sf PRF}
key
k\in{\{0,1\}}^{\kappa}
, a quantum state
\rho_{k}
and a master secret key
\mathsf{MSK}
.

•

{\sf PRF}(k,x)
: on input a key
k\in{\{0,1\}}^{\kappa}
and an input string
x\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
, it outputs a value
y\in\{0,1\}^{\ell^{\prime}}
. This is a deterministic algorithm.

•

{\sf Eval}(\rho_{k},x)
: on input a state
\rho_{k}
and an input
x\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
, it outputs a value
y\in\{0,1\}^{\ell^{\prime}}
.

•

\mathsf{Revoke}(\mathsf{MSK},\sigma)
: on input key
\mathsf{MSK}
and a state
\sigma
, it outputs
\mathsf{Valid}
or
\mathsf{Invalid}
.

We additionally require that the following holds:

Correctness.

For each
(k,\rho_{k},\mathsf{MSK})
in the support of
{\sf Gen}(1^{\lambda})
and for every
x\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
:

•

(Correctness of evaluation:)

{\sf Pr}\left[{\sf PRF}(k,x)={\sf Eval}(\rho_{k},x)\right]\geq 1-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

•

(Correctness of revocation:)

{\sf Pr}\left[\mathsf{Valid}\leftarrow\mathsf{Revoke}(\mathsf{MSK},\rho_{k})\right]\geq 1-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

9.2
Security

We define revocable
{\sf PRF}
security below.

\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathcal{A},\mu}(1^{\lambda},b)
:

Initialization Phase
:

•

The challenger computes
(k,\rho_{k},\mathsf{MSK})\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})
and sends
\rho_{k}
to
\mathcal{A}
.

Revocation Phase
:

•

The challenger sends the message
REVOKE
to
\mathcal{A}
.

•

The adversary
\mathcal{A}
sends a state
\sigma
to the challenger.

•

The challenger aborts if
\mathsf{Revoke}\left(\mathsf{MSK},\sigma\right)
outputs
\mathsf{Invalid}
.

Guessing Phase
:

•

The challenger samples bit
b\leftarrow\{0,1\}
.

•

The challenger samples random inputs
x_{1},\dots,x_{\mu}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}{\{0,1\}}^{\ell}
and then sends the values
(x_{1},\dots,x_{\mu})
and
(y_{1},\dots,y_{\mu})
to
\mathcal{A}
, where:

–

If
b=0
, set
y_{1}=\mathsf{PRF}(k,x_{1}),\,\dots,\,y_{\mu}=\mathsf{PRF}(k,x_{\mu})
and,

–

If
b=1
, set
y_{1},\dots,y_{\mu}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\{0,1\}^{\ell^{\prime}}
.

•

\mathcal{A}
outputs a bit
b^{\prime}
and wins if
b^{\prime}=b
.

Figure 21:
Revocable
\mathsf{PRF}
security

Definition 9.3

(Revocable
{\sf PRF}
Security)
.

A revocable pseudorandom function
(\mathsf{rPRF})
consisting of a tuple of
\mathsf{QPT}
algorithms
({\sf Gen},{\sf PRF},{\sf Eval},\mathsf{Revoke})
has
(\varepsilon,\delta,\mu)
revocable
\mathsf{PRF}
security if, for every
\mathsf{QPT}
adversary
\mathcal{A}
with

{\sf Pr}[\mathsf{Invalid}\leftarrow\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathcal{A},\mu}(1^{\lambda},b)]\leq\delta(\lambda)

for
b\in{\{0,1\}}
, it holds that

\left|\Pr\left[1\leftarrow\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathcal{A},\mu}(1^{\lambda},0)\right]-\Pr\left[1\leftarrow\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathcal{A},\mu}(1^{\lambda},1)\right]\right|\leq\varepsilon(\lambda),

where
\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathcal{A},\mu}
is as defined in
Figure

21
. If
\delta(\lambda)=1-1/\mathrm{poly}(\lambda)
,
\varepsilon(\lambda)=\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
we oftentimes drop
(\delta,\varepsilon)
and simply refer to it as
\mathsf{rPRF}
satisfies
\mu
-revocable
\mathsf{PRF}
security.

From one-query to multi-query security.

We show that proving security with respect to
\mu=1
is sufficient. That is, we show the following.

Claim 9.4
.

Supoose an
\mathsf{rPRF}
scheme

({\sf Gen},{\sf PRF},{\sf Eval},\mathsf{Revoke})
satisfies
1
-revocable
\mathsf{PRF}
security. Then,
\mathsf{rPRF}
also satisfies the stronger notion of (multi-query) revocable PRF security.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
\mathsf{QPT}
adversary that participating in the revocable
\mathsf{PRF}
security experiment defined in
Figure

21
and let
(x_{1},y_{1}),\ldots,(x_{\mu},y_{\mu})
denote the challenge input-output pairs, for some polynomial
\mu=\mu(\lambda)
. In the following, we denote by
k
the
\mathsf{PRF}
key sampled using
{\sf Gen}
by the challenger in
Figure

21
. We consider a sequence of hybrids defined as follows.

\mathsf{H}_{i}
, for
i\in[\mu+1]
: In this hybrid,
y_{1},\ldots,y_{i-1}
are sampled uniformly at random from
\{0,1\}^{\ell^{\prime}}
and
y_{i},\ldots,y_{\mu}
are generated as follows:
y_{j}={\sf PRF}(k,x_{j})
for
j\geq i
.

We claim that
\mathcal{A}
cannot distinguish between hybrids
\mathsf{H}_{i}
and
\mathsf{H}_{i+1}
, for all
i\in[\mu]
, with more than negligible advantage. Suppose for the sake of contradiction that the claim is not true, and that
\mathcal{A}
can distinguish
\mathsf{H}_{i}
and
\mathsf{H}_{i+1}
, for some index
i\in[\mu]
, with advantage at least
\varepsilon(\lambda)=1/\mathrm{poly}(\lambda)
. We will now show that we can use the adversary
\mathcal{A}
to break the 1-revocation security of
\mathsf{rPRF}
.

Consider a reduction
{\mathcal{B}}
that does the following:

1.

Receive the state
\rho_{k}
from the challenger.

2.

Sample
x_{i+1},\ldots,x_{\mu}
uniformly at random from
\{0,1\}^{\ell}
. Denote
\rho_{k}^{(i+1)}=\rho_{k}
. Do the following for
j=i+1,\ldots,\mu
:
{\sf Eval}(\rho_{k}^{(j)},x_{j})
to obtain
y_{j}
. Using the “Almost As Good As New”
[
Aar16
]
, recover
\rho_{k}^{(j+1)}
, where
\rho_{k}^{(j+1)}
is negligibly
18
18

18

Technically, this depends on the correctness error and we start with a
\mathsf{rPRF}
that is correct with probability negligibly close to 1.
close to
\rho_{k}
in trace distance.

3.

Forward the state
\rho_{k}^{(\mu+1)}
to
\mathcal{A}
.

4.

When the challenger submits the message
REVOKE
, forward the same message to
\mathcal{A}
.

5.

If
\mathcal{A}
sends
\sigma
, then forward the same state
\sigma
to the challenger.

6.

If the revocation did not fail, the guessing phase begins. The challenger sends
(x^{*},y^{*})
. Then, sample
x_{1},\ldots,x_{i-1}
uniformly at random from
\{0,1\}^{\ell}
and
y_{1},\ldots,y_{i-1}
uniformly at random from
\{0,1\}^{\ell^{\prime}}
. Set
x_{i}=x^{*}
and
y_{i}=y^{*}
. Send
(x_{1},y_{1}),\ldots,(x_{\mu},y_{\mu})
to
\mathcal{A}
.

7.

Output
b
, where
b
is the output of
\mathcal{A}
.

From the quantum union bound (
Lemma

2.3
), the “Almost As Good As New” lemma (
Lemma

2.2
) and the correctness of
\mathsf{rPRF}
, it follows that
\TD(\rho_{k},\rho_{k}^{(\mu+1)})\leq\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
and thus, the advantage of
\mathcal{A}
when given
\rho_{k}^{(\mu+1)}
instead of
\rho_{k}
is now at least
\varepsilon-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
. Moreover, by the design of
{\mathcal{B}}
, it follows that the success probability of
{\mathcal{B}}
in breaking 1-revocation security of
\mathsf{rPRF}
is exactly the same as the success probability of
\mathcal{A}
in breaking revocation security of
\mathsf{rPRF}
. This contradicts the fact that
\mathsf{rPRF}
satisfies 1-revocation security.

∎

Remark 9.5
.

Our notion of revocable
\mathsf{PRF}
security from
Definition

9.3
does not directly imply traditional notion of
\mathsf{pqPRF}
security
19
19

19

Although any revocable
\mathsf{PRF}
is a
weak
PRF. Recall that a weak PRF is one where the adversary receives as input
(x_{1},y_{1}),\ldots,(x_{\mu},y_{\mu})
, where
x_{i}
s are picked uniformly at random. The goal of the adversary is to distinguish the two cases: all
y_{i}
s are pseudorandom or all
y_{i}
s are picked uniformly at random.
from
Definition

9.1
. The reason is that the definition does not preclude the possibility of there being an input
x
(say an all zeroes string) on which,
\mathsf{PRF}
outputs
x
itself (or the first bit of
x
if the output of
\mathsf{PRF}
is a single bit).

Motivated by
Remark

9.5
, we now introduce the following notion of a
strong

\mathsf{rPRF}
.

Definition 9.6

(Strong
\mathsf{rPRF}
)
.

We say that a scheme

({\sf Gen},{\sf PRF},{\sf Eval},\mathsf{Revoke})
is a strong revocable pseudorandom function (or, strong
\mathsf{rPRF}
) if the following two properties hold:

1.

({\sf Gen},{\sf PRF},{\sf Eval},\mathsf{Revoke})
satisfy revocable
\mathsf{PRF}
security according to
Definition

9.3
, and

2.

({\sf Gen},\mathsf{PRF})
satisfy
\mathsf{pqPRF}
security according to
Definition

9.1
.

Remark 9.7
.

When instantiating pseudorandom functions in the textbook construction of private-key encryption
[
Gol06
]
from revocable pseudorandom functions, we immediately obtain a revocable private-key encryption scheme.

We show that the issue raised in
Remark

9.5
is not inherent. In fact, we give a simple generic transformation that allows us to obtain strong
\mathsf{rPRF}
s by making use of traditional
\mathsf{pqPRF}
s.

Claim 9.8

(Generic Transformation for Strong
\mathsf{rPRF}
s)
.

Let
({\sf Gen},{\sf PRF},{\sf Eval},\mathsf{Revoke})
be an
\mathsf{rPRF}
scheme which satisfies revocable
\mathsf{PRF}
security, and let

(\overline{{\sf Gen}},\overline{{\sf PRF}})
be a
\mathsf{pqPRF}
.
Then, the scheme
(\widetilde{{\sf Gen}},\widetilde{{\sf PRF}},\widetilde{{\sf Eval}},\widetilde{\mathsf{Revoke}})
is a strong
\mathsf{rPRF}
which consists of the following algorithms:

•

\widetilde{{\sf Gen}}(1^{\lambda})
: on input the security parameter
1^{\lambda}
, first run
(k,\rho_{k},\mathsf{MSK})\leftarrow{\sf Gen}(1^{\lambda})
and then output
((K,k),(K,\rho_{k}),\mathsf{MSK})
, where
K\leftarrow\overline{{\sf Gen}}(1^{\lambda})
is a
\mathsf{pqPRF}
key.

•

\widetilde{{\sf PRF}}((K,k),x)
: on input a key
(K,k)
and string
x\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
, output
\overline{{\sf PRF}}(K,x)\oplus\mathsf{PRF}(k,x)
.

•

\widetilde{{\sf Eval}}((K,\rho_{k}),x)
: on input
(K,\rho_{k})
and
x\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
, output
\overline{{\sf PRF}}(K,x)\oplus\mathsf{Eval}(\rho_{k},x)
.

•

\widetilde{\mathsf{Revoke}}(\mathsf{MSK},(K,\sigma))
: on input a master secret key
\mathsf{MSK}
and a pair
(K,\rho_{k})
, first discard the key
K
and then run
\mathsf{Revoke}(\mathsf{MSK},\sigma)
.

Proof.

Let us first show that the scheme
(\widetilde{{\sf Gen}},\widetilde{{\sf PRF}},\widetilde{{\sf Eval}},\widetilde{\mathsf{Revoke}})
maintains revocable
\mathsf{PRF}
security.
Suppose that there exists a
\mathsf{QPT}
adversary
\mathcal{A}
and a polynomial
\mu=\mu(\lambda)\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\left|\Pr\left[1\leftarrow\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathcal{A},\mu}(1^{\lambda},0)\right]-\Pr\left[1\leftarrow\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathcal{A},\mu}(1^{\lambda},1)\right]\right|=\epsilon(\lambda),

for some function
\epsilon(\lambda)=1/\mathrm{poly}(\lambda)
, and where
\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathcal{A},\mu}
is the experiment from
Figure

21
with respect to the scheme
(\widetilde{{\sf Gen}},\widetilde{{\sf PRF}},\widetilde{{\sf Eval}},\widetilde{\mathsf{Revoke}})
. We show that this implies the existence of a
\mathsf{QPT}
distinguisher
\mathcal{D}
that breaks the revocable
\mathsf{PRF}
security of the scheme
({\sf Gen},{\sf PRF},{\sf Eval},\mathsf{Revoke})
.

The distinguisher
\mathcal{D}
proceeds as follows:

1.

\mathcal{D}
receives as input a quantum state
\rho_{k}
, where
(k,\rho_{k},\mathsf{MSK})\leftarrow{\sf Gen}(1^{\lambda})
is generated by the challenger. Then,
\mathcal{D}
generates a
\mathsf{pqPRF}
key
K\leftarrow\overline{{\sf Gen}}(1^{\lambda})
and sends
(K,\rho_{k})
to
\mathcal{A}
.

2.

When
\mathcal{A}
returns a state
\rho
,
\mathcal{D}
forwards it to the challenger as part of the revocation phase.

3.

When
\mathcal{D}
receives the challenge input
(x_{1},\dots,x_{\mu})
and
(y_{1},\dots,y_{\mu})
from the challenger,
\mathcal{D}
sends
(x_{1},\dots,x_{\mu})
and
(\overline{{\sf PRF}}(K,x_{1})\oplus y_{1},\dots,\overline{{\sf PRF}}(K,x_{\mu})\oplus y_{\mu})
to
\mathcal{A}
.

4.

When
\mathcal{A}
outputs
b^{\prime}
, so does the distinguisher
\mathcal{D}
.

Note that the simulated challenge distribution above precisely matches the challenge distribution from the experiment
\mathsf{Expt}_{\mathcal{A},\mu}
from
Figure

21
. Therefore, if
\mathcal{A}
succeeds with inverse polynomial advantage
\epsilon(\lambda)=1/\mathrm{poly}(\lambda)
, so does
\mathcal{D}
– thereby breaking the revocable
\mathsf{PRF}
security of the scheme
({\sf Gen},{\sf PRF},{\sf Eval},\mathsf{Revoke})
. Consequently,
(\widetilde{{\sf Gen}},\widetilde{{\sf PRF}},\widetilde{{\sf Eval}},\widetilde{\mathsf{Revoke}})
satisfies revocable
\mathsf{PRF}
security.

To see why
(\widetilde{{\sf Gen}},\widetilde{\mathsf{PRF}})
satisfy
\mathsf{pqPRF}
security according to
Definition

9.1
, we can follow a similar argument as above to break the
\mathsf{pqPRF}
security of
(\overline{{\sf Gen}},\overline{{\sf PRF}})
. Here, we rely on the fact that the keys
(k,\rho_{k},\mathsf{MSK})\leftarrow{\sf Gen}(1^{\lambda})
and
K\leftarrow\overline{{\sf Gen}}(1^{\lambda})
are sampled independently from another.

∎

9.3
Construction

We construct a
\mathsf{PRF}
satisfying 1-revocation security (
Definition

9.3
).

Shift-Hiding Construction.

We construct a
shift-hiding
function which is loosely inspired by shift-hiding shiftable functions introduced by Peikert and Shiehian
[
PS18
]
.

Let
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
,
q\in\mathbb{N}
be a modulus and let
\ell=nm\lceil\log q\rceil
. In the following, we consider matrix-valued functions

F:\{0,1\}^{\ell}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
, where
F
is one of the following functions:

•

{\mathcal{Z}}:\{0,1\}^{\ell}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
which, on input
x\in{\{0,1\}}^{\ell}
, outputs an all zeroes matrix
\mathbf{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
, or:

•

H_{r}:\{0,1\}^{\ell}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
which, on input
x\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
, outputs
\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
, where
r\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
and
x=r\oplus{\sf bindecomp}(\mathbf{M})
, where
\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
and
{\sf bindecomp}(\cdot)
takes as input a matrix and outputs a binary string that is obtained by concatenating the binary decompositions of all the elements in the matrix (in some order).

We show that there exist
\mathsf{PPT}
algorithms
({\mathcal{K}}{\mathcal{G}},{\mathcal{E}})
(formally defined in
4
) with the following properties:

•

{\mathcal{K}}{\mathcal{G}}(1^{n},1^{m},q,\mathbf{A},F)
: on input
1^{n},1^{m}
, a modulus
q\in\mathbb{N}
, a matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
and a function
F\in\{\mathcal{Z}\}\cup\{H_{r}:r\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}\}
, it outputs a pair of keys
(pk_{F},sk_{F})
.

•

{\mathcal{E}}(pk_{F},x)
: on input
pk_{F}
,
x\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
, it outputs
{\bf S}_{x}\mathbf{A}+{\bf E}_{x}+F(x)
, where
{\bf S}_{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times n}
and
{\bf E}_{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
, where
||{\bf E}_{x}||\leq nm^{2}\sigma\lceil\log(q)\rceil
. Moreover, there is an efficient algorithm that recovers
{\bf S}_{x}
given
sk_{F}
and
x
.

We show that our construction of
({\mathcal{K}}{\mathcal{G}},{\mathcal{E}})
satisfies a

shift-hiding property
; namely, for any
r\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
,

\{pk_{{\mathcal{Z}}}\}\approx_{c}\{pk_{H_{r}}\},

for any
pk_{F}
with
(pk_{F},sk_{F})\leftarrow{\mathcal{K}}{\mathcal{G}}(1^{n},1^{m},q,\mathbf{A},F)
, where
\mathbf{A}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
, and
F\in\{\mathcal{Z},H_{r}\}
.

In the construction below, we consider a bijective function
\phi:[n]\times[m]\times[\lceil\log(q)\rceil]\rightarrow[\ell]
.

Construction 4
.

Consider the
\mathsf{PPT}
algorithms
({\mathcal{K}}{\mathcal{G}},{\mathcal{E}})
defined as follows:

•

{\mathcal{K}}{\mathcal{G}}(1^{n},1^{m},q,\mathbf{A},F)
: on input
1^{n},1^{m}
, a modulus
q\in\mathbb{N}
, a matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
and function
F\in\{\mathcal{Z}\}\cup\{H_{r}:r\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}\}
, it outputs a pair of keys
\kappa_{F}=(pk_{F},sk_{F})
generated as follows:

1.

For every
i,j\in[n],\tau\in[\lceil\log(q)\rceil]
, define
\{{\sf M}_{b}^{(i,j,\tau)}\}
as follows:

–

If
F={\mathcal{Z}}
, then for every
i\in[n],j\in[m],\tau\in[\lceil\log(q)\rceil]
, let
{\sf M}_{b}^{(i,j,\tau)}=\mathbf{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times n}
,

–

If
F=H_{r}
, then for every
i\in[n],j\in[m],\tau\in[\lceil\log(q)\rceil]
, let
{\sf M}_{b}^{(i,j,\tau)}=(b\oplus r_{\phi(i,j,\tau)})\cdot{\bf I}_{n\times n}
.

2.

For every
i\in[n],j\in[m],\tau\in[\lceil\log(q)\rceil]
,
b\in\{0,1\}
, compute:

pk_{b}^{(i,j,\tau)}={\bf S}_{b}^{(i,j,\tau)}\mathbf{A}+{\bf E}_{b}^{(i,j,\tau)}+{\sf M}_{b}^{(i,j,\tau)},

sk_{b}^{(i,j,\tau)}=\left(\left\{{\bf S}_{b}^{(i,j,\tau)},{\bf E}_{b}^{(i,j,\tau)}\right\}\right),

where for every
i\in[n],j\in[m],\tau\in[\lceil\log(q)\rceil]
,
b\in\{0,1\}
:

–

{\bf S}_{b}^{(i,j,\tau)}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z}_{q},\sigma}^{n\times n}
,

–

{\bf E}_{b}^{(i,j,\tau)}\leftarrow D_{\mathbb{Z}_{q},\sigma}^{n\times m}

3.

Output
pk_{F}=\left(\mathbf{A},\left\{pk^{(i,j,\tau)}_{b}\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[n],j\in[m],\\
\tau\in[\lceil\log(q)\rceil],b\in\{0,1\}\end{subarray}}\right)
and
sk_{F}=\left\{sk^{(i,j,\tau)}_{b}\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[n],j\in[m],\\
\tau\in[\lceil\log(q)\rceil],b\in\{0,1\}\end{subarray}}
.

•

{\mathcal{E}}(pk_{F},x)
: on input
pk_{F}
and
x\in{\{0,1\}}^{\ell}
, proceed as follows:

1.

Parse
pk_{F}=\left(\mathbf{A}\left\{pk_{b}^{(i,j,\tau)}\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[n],j\in[m],\\
\tau\in[\lceil\log(q)\rceil],b\in\{0,1\}\end{subarray}}\right)

2.

Output
\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[n],j\in[m],\\
\tau\in[\lceil\log(q)\rceil]\end{subarray}}pk_{x_{\phi(i,j,\tau)}}^{(i,j,\tau)}
.

Claim 9.9

(Correctness)
.

Let
({\mathcal{K}}{\mathcal{G}},{\mathcal{E}})
be the pair of
\mathsf{PPT}
algorithms in
4
. Let
(pk_{F},sk_{F})\leftarrow{\mathcal{K}}{\mathcal{G}}(1^{n},1^{m},q,\mathbf{A},F)
with
F\in\{\mathcal{Z}\}\cup\{H_{r}:r\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}\}
. Then, the output of
{\mathcal{E}}(pk_{F},x)
is of the form:

{\mathcal{E}}(pk_{F},x)={\bf S}_{x}\mathbf{A}+{\bf E}_{x}+F(x),

where
{\bf S}_{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times n}
and
{\bf E}_{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
with
||{\bf E}_{x}||\leq nm^{2}\sigma\lceil\log(q)\rceil
. Moreover, there is an efficient algorithm that recovers
{\bf S}_{x}
given
(pk_{F},sk_{F})
.

Proof.

Let
(pk_{F},sk_{F})\leftarrow{\mathcal{K}}{\mathcal{G}}(1^{n},1^{m},q,\mathbf{A},F)
. Parse
pk_{F}=\left(\mathbf{A},\left\{pk^{(i,j,\tau)}_{b}\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[n],j\in[m],\\
\tau\in[\lceil\log(q)\rceil],b\in\{0,1\}\end{subarray}}\right)
and
sk_{F}=\left\{sk^{(i,j,\tau)}_{b}\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[n],j\in[m],\\
\tau\in[\lceil\log(q)\rceil],b\in\{0,1\}\end{subarray}}
, where:

pk_{b}^{(i,j,\tau)}={\bf S}_{b}^{(i,j,\tau)}\mathbf{A}+{\bf E}_{b}^{(i,j,\tau)}+{\sf M}_{b}^{(i,j,\tau)},

sk_{b}^{(i,j,\tau)}=\left(\{{\bf S}_{b}^{(i,j,\tau)},{\bf E}_{b}^{(i,j,\tau)}\}\right)

There are two cases to consider here:

Case 1
.
F={\mathcal{Z}}
: in this case,
{\sf M}_{b}^{(i,j,\tau)}=\bf 0
, for every
i\in[n],j\in[m],\tau\in[\lceil\log(q)\rceil],b\in\{0,1\}
. Thus, the following holds:

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[n],j\in[m],\\
\tau\in[\lceil\log(q)\rceil]\end{subarray}}pk_{x_{\phi(i,j,\tau)}}^{(i,j,\tau)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underbrace{\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[n],j\in[m],\\
\tau\in[\lceil\log(q)\rceil]\end{subarray}}{\bf S}_{x_{\phi(i,j,\tau)}}^{(i,j,\tau)}\right)}_{{\bf S}_{x}}\mathbf{A}+\underbrace{\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[n],j\in[m],\\
\tau\in[\lceil\log(q)\rceil]\end{subarray}}{\bf E}_{x_{\phi(i,j,\tau)}}^{(i,j,\tau)}\right)}_{{\bf E}_{x}}+\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[n],j\in[m],\\
\tau\in[\lceil\log(q)\rceil]\end{subarray}}{\sf M}_{x_{\phi(i,j,\tau)}}^{(i,j,\tau)}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\bf S}_{x}\mathbf{A}+{\bf E}_{x}+{\mathcal{Z}}(x)

Moreover, we have that
||{\bf E}_{b}^{(i,j,\tau)}||\leq m\sigma
and thus,
||{\bf E}_{x}||\leq nm^{2}\sigma\lceil\log(q)\rceil
.

Case 2
.
F={H}_{r}
:

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[n],j\in[m],\\
\tau\in[\lceil\log(q)\rceil]\end{subarray}}pk_{x_{\phi(i,j,\tau)}}^{(i,j,\tau)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\bf S}_{x}\mathbf{A}+{\bf E}_{x}+\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[n],j\in[m],\\
\tau\in[\lceil\log(q)\rceil]\end{subarray}}{\sf M}_{x_{\phi(i,j,\tau)}}^{(i,j,\tau)}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\bf S}_{x}\mathbf{A}+{\bf E}_{x}+H_{r}(x),

where
{\bf S}_{x}
and
{\bf E}_{x}
are as defined above. The second equality holds because of the fact that
{\sf M}_{x_{\phi(i,j,\tau)}}^{(i,j,\tau)}
has the value
(b\oplus r_{\phi(i,j,\tau)})\cdot 2^{\tau}
in the
(i,j)^{th}
position and zero, everywhere else. Thus, summing up all the
{\sf M}_{x_{\phi(i,j,\tau)}}^{(i,j,\tau)}
matrices results in the matrix
{\sf M}
, where
x\oplus r
is the binary decomposition of
{\sf M}
.

Finally, it is clear that
{\bf S}_{x}
can be efficiently recovered from
sk_{F}
and
x
.
∎

Claim 9.10

(Shift-hiding property)
.

Assuming the quantum hardness of learning with errors, the pair
({\mathcal{K}}{\mathcal{G}},{\mathcal{E}})
in
4
has the property that

\{pk_{{\mathcal{Z}}}\}\approx_{c}\{pk_{H_{r}}\},

for any
pk_{F}
with
(pk_{F},sk_{F})\leftarrow{\mathcal{K}}{\mathcal{G}}(1^{n},1^{m},q,\mathbf{A},F)
, where
\mathbf{A}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
,
r\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
and
F\in\{\mathcal{Z},H_{r}\}
.

Proof.

For every
i\in[n],j\in[m],\tau\in[\lceil\log(q)\rceil]
,
b\in\{0,1\}
, let
{\sf M}_{b}^{(i,j,\tau)}=(b\oplus r_{\phi(i,j,\tau)})\cdot{\bf I}_{n\times n}
. Then from the quantum hardness of learning with errors, the following holds for every
(i,j,\tau)
and
b\in\{0,1\}
:

\{{\bf S}_{b}^{(i,j,\tau)}\mathbf{A}+{\bf E}_{b}^{(i,j,\tau)}\}\approx_{c}\{{\bf S}_{b}^{(i,j,\tau)}\mathbf{A}+{\bf E}_{b}^{(i,j,\tau)}+{\sf M}_{b}^{(i,j,\tau)}\}

Since
\{{\bf S}_{b}^{(i,j,\tau)}\}
and
\{{\bf E}_{b}^{(i,j,\tau)}\}
are sampled independently for every
(i,j,\tau)
and
b\in\{0,1\}
, the proof of the claim follows.
∎

Remark 9.11
.

When consider the all-zeroes function
{\mathcal{Z}}
, we drop the notation from the parameters. For instance, we denote
pk_{{\mathcal{Z}}}
to be simply
pk
.

Construction.

We consider the following parameters which are relevant to our
{\sf PRF}
construction. Let
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
and let
q\in\mathbb{N}
be a modulus with
q=2^{o(n)}
, and let
\ell=nm\lceil\log q\rceil
. Let
\sigma
be a parameter with
\sqrt{8m}<\sigma<q/\sqrt{8m}
and let
p\ll q
be a sufficiently large rounding parameter with

n\cdot m^{3}\sigma^{2}\lceil\log q\rceil=(q/p)\cdot 2^{-o(n)}.

We now construct a key-revocable Dual-Regev (leveled) fully homomorphic encryption scheme based on our key-revocable Dual-Regev public-key scheme.

Remark 9.12
.

While the construction in this section can be readily adapted to feature
classical revocation
via
2
, we choose to focus on
quantum revocation
for simplicity by making use of our Dual-Regev scheme from
1
.

We describe our construction below.

Construction 5

(Revocable
{\sf PRF}
scheme)
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
be the security parameter and
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
q\geq 2
be a prime and let
\sigma>0
be a parameter. Let

({\mathcal{K}}{\mathcal{G}},{\mathcal{E}})
be the procedure in
4
.
Our revocable
{\sf PRF}
scheme is defined as follows:

•

{\sf Gen}(1^{\lambda})
: This is the following key generation procedure:

1.

Sample
(\mathbf{A},\mathsf{td}_{\mathbf{A}})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenTrap}(1^{n},1^{m},q)
.

2.

Compute
\kappa_{{\mathcal{Z}}}\leftarrow{\mathcal{K}}{\mathcal{G}}(1^{n},1^{m},q,\mathbf{A},{\mathcal{Z}})
, where
{\mathcal{Z}}:\{0,1\}^{\ell}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
is the such that
{\mathcal{Z}}(x)
outputs an all zero matrix for every
x\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
. Parse
\kappa_{{\mathcal{Z}}}
as
(pk,sk)
.

3.

Generate a Gaussian superposition
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)
with

\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}\,\,=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}\\
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{y}\end{subarray}}\rho_{\sigma}(\mathbf{x})\,\ket{\mathbf{x}}.

Output
k=(pk,sk,\mathbf{y})
,
\rho_{k}=(pk,\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}})
and
\mathsf{MSK}=\mathsf{td}_{\mathbf{A}}
.

•

{\sf PRF}(k,x)
: this is the following procedure:

1.

Parse the key
k
as a tuple
(pk,sk),\mathbf{y})
.

2.

Output
\lfloor{\bf S}_{x}\mathbf{y}\rceil_{p}
. Here,
{\bf S}_{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times n}
is a matrix that can be efficiently recovered from
sk
as stated in
9.9
.

•

{\sf Eval}(\rho_{k},x)
: this is the following evaluation algorithm:

1.

Parse
\rho_{k}
as
(pk,\rho)
.

2.

Compute
{\sf M}_{x}\leftarrow{\mathcal{E}}(pk,x)
.

3.

Measure the register
{\sf Aux}
of the state
U(\rho\otimes|0\rangle\langle 0|_{\sf Aux})U^{\dagger}
. Denote the resulting outcome to be
{\bf z}
, where
U
is defined as follows:

U\ket{\mathbf{t}}\ket{0}_{\sf Aux}\rightarrow\ket{\mathbf{t}}\ket{\lfloor{\sf M}_{x}\cdot\mathbf{t}\rceil_{p}}_{\sf Aux}

4.

Output
{\bf z}
.

•

\mathsf{Revoke}(\mathsf{MSK},\rho)
: given as input the trapdoor
\mathsf{td}_{\mathbf{A}}\leftarrow\mathsf{MSK}
, apply the projective measurement
\{\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}\!\bra{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},I-\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}\!\bra{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}\}
onto the state
\rho
using the procedure
\mathsf{QSampGauss}(\mathbf{A},\mathsf{td}_{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{y},\sigma)
in Algorithm
2
. Output
\mathsf{Valid}
if the measurement is successful, and
\mathsf{Invalid}
otherwise.

Lemma 9.13
.

The above scheme satisfies correctness for our choice of parameters.

Proof.

The correctness of revocation follows immediately from the correctness of
\mathsf{QSampGauss}
in Algorithm
2
, which we showed in
Theorem

3.3
.
Next, we show the correctness of evaluation. Let
\kappa_{{\mathcal{Z}}}\leftarrow{\mathcal{K}}{\mathcal{G}}(1^{n},1^{m},q,\mathbf{A},{\mathcal{Z}})
with
\kappa_{{\mathcal{Z}}}=(\mathsf{PK},\mathsf{SK})
.
From
9.9
, we have for any
x\in{\{0,1\}}^{\ell}
:

{\mathcal{E}}(\mathsf{PK},x)={\bf S}_{x}\mathbf{A}+{\bf E}_{x}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q),

where
{\bf S}_{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times n}
and
{\bf E}_{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
with
||{\bf E}_{x}||_{\infty}\leq nm^{2}\sigma\lceil\log(q)\rceil
. Recall that
\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)
outputs a state
\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}
that is overwhelmingly supported on vectors
\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
such that
\|\mathbf{t}\|\leq\sigma\sqrt{\frac{m}{2}}
with
\mathbf{A}\cdot\mathbf{t}=\mathbf{y}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q)
. Therefore, we have for any input
x\in{\{0,1\}}^{\ell}
:

\left\lfloor{\mathcal{E}}(\mathsf{PK},x)\cdot\mathbf{t}\right\rceil_{p}=\left\lfloor{\bf S}_{x}\mathbf{A}\cdot\mathbf{t}+{\bf E}_{x}\cdot\mathbf{t}\right\rceil_{p}=\left\lfloor{\bf S}_{x}\cdot\mathbf{y}+{\bf E}_{x}\cdot\mathbf{t}\right\rceil_{p}=\left\lfloor{\bf S}_{x}\cdot\mathbf{y}\right\rceil_{p},

where the last equality follows from the fact that

\|\mathbf{E}_{x}\cdot\mathbf{t}\|_{2}\leq\|\mathbf{E}_{x}\|_{2}\cdot\|\mathbf{t}\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{m}\cdot\|\mathbf{E}_{x}\|\cdot\|\mathbf{t}\|_{2}\leq n\sqrt{m}m^{2}\sigma\lceil\log(q)\rceil\cdot\sigma\sqrt{m/2}.

and
n\cdot m^{3}\sigma^{2}\lceil\log q\rceil=(q/p)\cdot 2^{-o(n)}
for our choice of parameters.
∎

Proof of security.

Our first result on the security of
5
concerns
(\mathsf{negl}(\lambda),\mathsf{negl}(\lambda),1)
-security, i.e., we assume that revocation succeeds with overwhelming probability.

Theorem 9.14
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
q
be a prime modulus with
q=2^{o(n)}
and
m\geq 2n\log q
, each parameterized by
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\ell=nm\lceil\log q\rceil
. Let
\sqrt{8m}<\sigma<q/\sqrt{8m}
and
\alpha\in(0,1)
be any noise ratio with
1/\alpha=\sigma\cdot 2^{o(n)}
, and let
p\ll q
be a sufficiently large rounding parameter with

n\cdot m^{3}\sigma^{2}\lceil\log q\rceil=(q/p)\cdot 2^{-o(n)}.

Then, assuming the quantum subexponential hardness of
\mathsf{LWE}_{n,q,\alpha q}^{m}

and
\mathsf{SIS}_{n,q,\sigma\sqrt{2m}}^{m}
, our revocable
\mathsf{PRF}
scheme

({\sf Gen},{\sf PRF},{\sf Eval},\mathsf{Revoke})
defined in
5
satisfies
(\mathsf{negl}(\lambda),\mathsf{negl}(\lambda),1)
-revocation security according to
Definition

9.3
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
\mathsf{QPT}
adversary and suppose that

\vline\,\Pr\left[1\leftarrow\mathsf{Expt}^{\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda},0)\ \right]-\Pr\left[1\leftarrow\mathsf{Expt}^{\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda},1)\ \right]\,\vline=\epsilon(\lambda),

for some function
\varepsilon(\lambda)
with respect to security experiment
\mathsf{Expt}^{\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda},b)
from
Figure

22
. To complete the proof, it suffices to show that
\varepsilon(\lambda)
is negligible.

\mathsf{Expt}^{\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda},b)
:

Initialization Phase
:

•

The challenger runs the procedure
\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})
:

1.

Sample
(\mathbf{A},\mathsf{td}_{\mathbf{A}})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenTrap}(1^{n},1^{m},q)
.

2.

Generate
\mathbf{A}_{N}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(n+m)\times m}
with
\overline{\mathbf{A}_{N}}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m\times m}
and
\underline{\mathbf{A}_{N}}=\mathbf{A}
.

3.

Compute
\kappa_{{\mathcal{Z}}}\leftarrow{\mathcal{K}}{\mathcal{G}}(1^{n},1^{m},1^{q},\mathbf{A}_{N},{\mathcal{Z}})
, where
{\mathcal{K}}{\mathcal{G}}
is as defined in
4
and
{\mathcal{Z}}:\{0,1\}^{\ell}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
is such that
{\mathcal{Z}}(x)
outputs an all zero matrix for every
x\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
. Parse
\kappa_{{\mathcal{Z}}}
as
(pk,sk)
.

4.

Generate
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)
with

\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}\,\,=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}\\
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{y}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ q)\end{subarray}}\rho_{\sigma}(\mathbf{x})\,\ket{\mathbf{x}}.

5.

Let
k=(pk,sk,\mathbf{y})
,
\rho_{k}=(pk,\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}})
and
\mathsf{MSK}=\mathsf{td}_{\mathbf{A}}
.

•

The challenger sends
\rho_{k}=(pk,\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}})
to
\mathcal{A}
.

Revocation Phase
:

•

The challenger sends the message
REVOKE
to
\mathcal{A}
.

•

\mathcal{A}
generates a (possibly entangled) bipartite quantum state
\rho_{R,\mathbf{\textsc{aux}}}
in systems
\mathcal{H}_{R}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\textsc{Aux}}
with
\mathcal{H}_{R}=\mathcal{H}_{q}^{m}
, returns system
R
and holds onto the auxiliary system
Aux
.

•

The challenger runs
\mathsf{Revoke}(\mathsf{MSK},\rho_{R})
, where
\rho_{R}
is the reduced state in system
R
. If the outcome is
\mathsf{Invalid}
, the challenger aborts.

Guessing Phase
:

•

The challenger samples
x\leftarrow{\{0,1\}}^{\ell}
and sends
(x,y)
to
\mathcal{A}
, where

–

If
b=0
: compute
{\bf S}_{x}
from
sk
as in
9.9
.
Set
y=\lfloor{\bf S}_{x}\mathbf{y}\rceil_{p}
.

–

If
b=1
: sample
y\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{n}
.

•

\mathcal{A}
outputs a string
b^{\prime}
and wins if
b^{\prime}=b
.

Figure 22:
The revocable
{\sf PRF}
experiment
\mathsf{Expt}^{\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda},b)
for
5
.

Suppose for the sake of contradition that
\epsilon(\lambda)=1/\mathrm{poly}(\lambda)
.
Let us now introduce a sequence of hybrid experiments which will be relevant for the remainder of the proof.

Let
\mathsf{RevDual}=(\mathsf{KeyGen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec},\mathsf{Revoke})
be the
n
-bit key-revocable Dual-Regev scheme from
1
. Fix
\mu=0^{n}
, where
\mu
is the challenge message in the dual-Regev encryption security.

\mathsf{H}_{0}
: This is
\mathsf{Expt}^{\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda},0)
in
Figure

22
.

\mathsf{H}_{1}
: This is the same experiment as
\mathsf{Expt}^{\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda},0)
, except for the following changes:

•

Sample a random string
r\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell}
.

•

Run the procedure
\mathsf{RevDual}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})
instead of
\mathsf{GenTrap}(1^{n},1^{m},q)
and
\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)
to obtain
(\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n},\mathsf{MSK},\rho_{\mathsf{SK}})
.

•

Compute
(\mathsf{CT}_{1},\mathsf{CT}_{2})\leftarrow\mathsf{RevDual}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\mu)
, where
\mathsf{CT}_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
and
\mathsf{CT}_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
.

•

Set
x=r\oplus{\sf bindecomp}(\mathsf{CT}_{1})
.

The rest of the hybrid is the same as before.

Note that Hybrids
\mathsf{H}_{0}
and
\mathsf{H}_{1}
are identically distributed.

\mathsf{H}_{2}
: This is the same experiment as before, except that the challenger now uses an alternative key-generation algorithm:

•

As before, run the procedure
\mathsf{RevDual}.\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\lambda})
instead of
\mathsf{GenTrap}(1^{n},1^{m},q)
and
\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)
to obtain
(\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n},\mathsf{MSK},\rho_{\mathsf{SK}})
. Sample
r\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell}
.

•

Let
H_{r}:\{0,1\}^{\ell}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
be as defined in the beginning of
Section

9.3
.

•

Run the alternate algorithm
\kappa_{H}\leftarrow{\mathcal{K}}{\mathcal{G}}(1^{n},1^{m},1^{q},\mathbf{A},H_{r})
instead of
\kappa_{{\mathcal{Z}}}\leftarrow{\mathcal{K}}{\mathcal{G}}(1^{n},1^{m},1^{q},\mathbf{A},{\mathcal{Z}})
.

•

Compute the ciphertext
(\mathsf{CT}^{*}_{1},\mathsf{CT}^{*}_{2})\leftarrow\mathsf{RevDual}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\mu)
, where
\mathsf{CT}^{*}_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
. Then, set
x^{*}=r\oplus{\sf bindecomp}(\mathsf{CT}_{1}^{*})
. Send
x^{*}
to the adversary in the guessing phase.

\mathsf{H}_{3}
: This is the same hybrid as before, except that we choose
\mathsf{CT}_{1}^{*}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
and
\mathsf{CT}_{2}^{*}\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
.

\mathsf{H}_{4}
: This is the
\mathsf{Expt}^{\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda},1)
in
Figure

22
.

Note that hybrids
\mathsf{H}_{3}
and
\mathsf{H}_{4}
are identically distributed.

We now show the following.

Claim 9.15
.

By the shift-hiding property of
({\mathcal{K}}{\mathcal{G}},{\mathcal{E}})
in
9.10
, we have that the two hybrids
\mathsf{H}_{1}
and
\mathsf{H}_{2}
are computationally indistinguishable, i.e.

\mathsf{H}_{1}\approx_{c}\mathsf{H}_{2}.

Proof.

Suppose for the sake of contradiction that there exists a non-negligble difference in the advantage of the adversary
\mathcal{A}
in the two hybrids
\mathsf{H}_{1}
and
\mathsf{H}_{2}
.

We now design a reduction
{\mathcal{B}}
that violates the shift-hiding property as follows.

1.

Sample
r\xleftarrow{\$}\{0,1\}^{\ell}
. Send
({\mathcal{Z}},H_{r})
to the challenger.

2.

The challenger responds with
pk=\left(\mathbf{A},\left\{\mathsf{CT}_{b}^{(i,j,\tau)}\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[n],j\in[m]\\
\tau\in[\lceil\log(q)\rceil],b\in\{0,1\}\end{subarray}}\right)

3.

Compute
(\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenGauss}(\mathbf{A},\sigma)
from the challenger.

4.

Set
\rho_{k}=(pk,\rho)
.

5.

Compute
(\mathsf{CT}_{1},\mathsf{CT}_{2})\leftarrow\mathsf{RevDual}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y},\mu)
, where
\mathsf{CT}_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
and
\mathsf{CT}_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
. Then, set
x^{*}=r\oplus{\sf bindecomp}(\mathsf{CT}_{1})
.

6.

Compute
{\sf Eval}(\rho_{k},x^{*})
to obtain
y^{*}
while recovering
\rho^{*}_{k}
(using the ”Almost as Good As New Lemma”,
Lemma

2.2
) such that
{\sf TD}(\rho^{*}_{k},\rho_{k})\leq\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
.

7.

Send
\rho_{k}^{*}
to
\mathcal{A}
.

8.

\mathcal{A}
computes a state on two registers
R
and
aux
. It returns the state on the register
R
.

9.

\mathcal{A}
, on input the register
aux
and
(x^{*},y^{*})
, outputs a bit
b^{\prime}
.

10.

Output
b^{\prime}
.

If
pk
is generated using
{\mathcal{K}}{\mathcal{G}}(1^{n},1^{m},q,\mathbf{A},{\mathcal{Z}})
then we are precisely in
\mathsf{H}_{1}
(except that
\mathsf{Revoke}
is not performed). Moreover, if
pk
is generated using
{\mathcal{K}}{\mathcal{G}}(1^{n},1^{m},q,\mathbf{A},H_{r})
then we are in the hybrid
\mathsf{H}_{2}
(except that
\mathsf{Revoke}
is not performed). Therefore
\mathcal{B}
has a non-negligible advantage at distinguishing
\mathsf{H}_{1}
and
\mathsf{H}_{2}
whenever
\mathsf{Revoke}
outputs
\top
on system
R
.
Using
Lemma

6.10
, this implies that we can break the shift-hiding property with non-negligible advantage. This proves the claim.
∎

Next, we invoke the security of the
n
-bit variant of our key-revocable Dual-Regev scheme (which is implied by
Theorem

6.1
) to show the following.

Claim 9.16
.

By the security of our
n
-bit key-revocable Dual-Regev encryption scheme, we have that the two hybrids
\mathsf{H}_{2}
and
\mathsf{H}_{3}
are computationally indistinguishable, i.e.

\mathsf{H}_{2}\approx_{c}\mathsf{H}_{3}.

Proof.

Suppose for the sake of contradiction that there exists a non-negligble difference in the advantage of
\mathcal{A}
in the two hybrids
\mathsf{H}_{2}
and
\mathsf{H}_{3}
. Using
\mathcal{A}
, we can now design a reduction
{\mathcal{B}}
that violates the revocation security of our
n
-bit revocable Dual-Regev scheme which is implicit in
Theorem

6.1
.

The reduction
{\mathcal{B}}
proceeds as follows.

1.

First, it receives as input
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{y}
and a quantum state

\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}\\
\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{y}\end{subarray}}\rho_{\sigma}(\mathbf{x})\ket{\mathbf{x}}.

2.

The reduction generates a quantum state
\rho_{k}
as follows:

•

Sample a random string
r\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\,\xleftarrow{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\$$}}\,$}{\{0,1\}}^{\ell}
.

•

Let
H_{r}:\{0,1\}^{\ell}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
be as defined in the beginning of
Section

9.3
.

•

Run the algorithm
\kappa_{H}\leftarrow{\mathcal{K}}{\mathcal{G}}(1^{n},1^{m},1^{q},\mathbf{A},H_{r})
and parse
\kappa_{H}
as
(pk,sk)
.

•

Set
\rho_{k}=(pk,\ket{\psi_{\mathbf{y}}})
.

Send
\rho_{k}
to
\mathcal{A}
.

3.

\mathcal{A}
outputs a state on two registers
R
and
aux
. The register
R
is returned. The reduction forwards the register
R
to the challenger.

4.

The reduction then gets the challenge ciphertext
\mathsf{CT}=[\mathsf{CT}_{1},\mathsf{CT}_{2}]^{\intercal}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}\times\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
. The reduction then sets

x^{*}=r\oplus{\sf bindecomp}(\mathsf{CT}_{1})

and sends
x^{*}
to
\mathcal{A}
in the guessing phase, together with
y=\lfloor{\bf S}_{x^{*}}\mathbf{y}+\mathsf{CT}_{2}\rceil_{p}

which is computed using the secret key
\mathsf{SK}
(c.f.
9.9
).

5.

\mathcal{A}
outputs a bit
b^{\prime}
.
{\mathcal{B}}
outputs
b^{\prime}
.

There are two cases to consider here. In the first case, we have
\mathsf{CT}=[\mathsf{CT}_{1},\mathsf{CT}_{2}]^{\intercal}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}\times\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
is a Dual-Regev ciphertext. Here,
y=\lfloor{\bf S}_{x^{*}}\mathbf{y}+\mathsf{CT}_{2}\rceil_{p}
precisely corresponds to the output of the pseudorandom function on
\rho_{k}
and
x
. In the second case, we have
\mathsf{CT}=[\mathsf{CT}_{1},\mathsf{CT}_{2}]^{\intercal}
, where
\mathsf{CT}_{1}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n\times m}
and
\mathsf{CT}_{2}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{m}
. Therefore, the resulting string
y=\lfloor{\bf S}_{x^{*}}\mathbf{y}+\mathsf{CT}_{2}\rceil_{p}
is negligibly close (in total variation distance) to a uniform distribution on
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{m}
.

Putting everything together, we find that the first case corresponds precisely to
\mathsf{H}_{2}
, whereas the second case corresponds to
\mathsf{H}_{3}
. As a result,
{\mathcal{B}}
violates the revocation security of our
n
-bit revocable Dual-Regev scheme which is implicit in
Theorem

6.1
.This completes the proof.
∎

Putting everything together, we have shown that

\vline\,\Pr\left[1\leftarrow\mathsf{Expt}^{\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda},0)\ \right]-\Pr\left[1\leftarrow\mathsf{Expt}^{\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda},1)\ \right]\,\vline\leq\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

∎

Finally, we prove that
5
achieves the stronger notion of
(\mathsf{negl}(\lambda),1-1/\mathrm{poly}(\lambda),1)
-security assuming
Theorem

6.16
.

Theorem 9.17
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
q
be a prime modulus with
q=2^{o(n)}
and
m\geq 2n\log q
, each parameterized by
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\ell=nm\lceil\log q\rceil
. Let
\sqrt{8m}<\sigma<q/\sqrt{8m}
and
\alpha\in(0,1)
be any noise ratio with
1/\alpha=\sigma\cdot 2^{o(n)}
, and let
p\ll q
be a sufficiently large rounding parameter with

n\cdot m^{3}\sigma^{2}\lceil\log q\rceil=(q/p)\cdot 2^{-o(n)}.

Then, assuming
Theorem

6.16
, our revocable
\mathsf{PRF}
scheme

({\sf Gen},{\sf PRF},{\sf Eval},\mathsf{Revoke})
defined in
5
has
(\mathsf{negl}(\lambda),1-1/\mathrm{poly}(\lambda),1)
-revocation security according to
Definition

9.3
.

Proof.

The proof is the same as in
Theorem

9.14
, except that we invoke
Theorem

6.2
instead of
Theorem

6.1
for Dual-Regev security.
∎
